Statistical inference for subgraph counts and clustering coefficient using network sampling in a sparse Stochastic Block Model framework
A. Mandal, A. Chatterjee

Abstract
This article develops limit laws for network sampling based estimates of subgraph counts and clustering coefficient of a large population network, and uses them for predictive inference. A model based approach is used, where the population network is assumed to be generated from a sparse Stochastic Block Model (SBM). In order to quantify the effects of node sampling under resource constraints, a sparse Bernoulli node sampling scheme is introduced, where the node selection probability is allowed to decay to zero as the population size increases. Both induced and ego-centric network formation approaches are explored. Quantitative bounds on the speed of normal approximation for estimated subgraph counts are obtained in a joint model and design based asymptotic framework, and these bounds show that the accuracy of statistical inference depends intricately on the level of sparsity in the model, the sparsity level of the sampling scheme, and on features like the edge density and minimum vertex cover size of the target subgraph. We find that the ego-centric approach can handle higher levels of sparsity in the model and the sampling scheme, compared to the induced approach. We also show that if the model sparsity level remains below an initial threshold, then the quality of statistical inference remains unaffected by the model sparsity level. Beyond this threshold, the quality degrades rapidly. The sufficient conditions for obtaining a Gaussian limit law also turn out to be necessary . For strictly balanced target subgraphs, we obtain sharp transitions from Gaussian to various types of Poisson based limit laws, as the model and sampling sparsity levels increase. A complete description of all possible limit laws for estimated subgraph counts is found in the induced case, and a near-complete description is obtained in the ego-centric case. As an application of our results on estimated subgraph counts, we obtain Gaussian and Poisson based limit laws for the estimated clustering coefficient, in both induced and ego-centric cases. A simulation study provides strong support for the theoretical results at various levels of model sparsity and sampling sparsity. Finally, the proposed methodology is applied to a real data set.



Keywords: Induced, Ego-centric, Prediction, Poisson limit, Sparse sampling, Minimum vertex cover.

AMS 2020 Subject Classification:
62E20 (Primary); 62D05 (Secondary).

1
Introduction

In recent years, network sampling has emerged as a practical and feasible method to observe data from large networks (
Newman (2018)
), especially in cases where the
entire
target network (population) is difficult to access (cf.
Kolaczyk (2009)
,
Zhang et al. (2015)
) or the entire data set is too large to use for storage and analysis (
Leskovec and Faloutsos (2006)
). Applied researchers are interested in accurate estimation of various population network level summary statistics using the sampled network data. However, a large amount of empirical research indicates that estimates obtained through network sampling can be severely biased, and the bias depends on the structure of the population network, the sampling mechanism, and the target summary statistic (cf.
Lee et al. (2006)
,
Bliss et al. (2014)
,
Ribeiro and Towsley (2012)
,
Ebbes et al. (2008)
,
González-Bailón et al. (2014)
,
Maiya and Berger-Wolf (2011)
,
Costenbader and Valente (2003)
,
Chandrasekhar and Lewis (2016)
,
Illenberger and Flötteröd (2012)
,
Ruggeri and De Bacco (2020)
,
Hsieh et al. (2024)
, and the references therein for a selective overview). In spite of extensive empirical research on network sampling, there has been a lack of rigorous theoretical results on the distributional properties of network sampling based estimates and their use for statistical inference about their population level counterparts. Initial investigations on network sampling have focused on a design based approach (cf.
Frank (1978b)
,
Frank (1978a)
,
Frank (2011)
) and model based approaches have been explored more recently (cf.
Thompson and Frank (2000)
,
Shi et al. (2019)
,
Handcock and Gile (2010)
and
Gile and Handcock (2015)
). Recently,
Bhattacharya et al. (2022)
made a seminal contribution in this area and proved a CLT for estimated subgraph counts under a Bernoulli node sampling scheme (using induced network formation) in a design based framework, assuming that the population network is generated
non-stochastically
. Similar inferential questions arise in a model based setting, where the population network is generated stochastically. The question is relevant due to the extensive use of stochastic models in network modeling (see
Newman (2018)
,
Kolaczyk (2009)
,
van der Hofstad (2017)
,
Fienberg (2012)
,
Izenman (2023)
). To the best of our knowledge, this question has not been explored in the existing literature. Neither exist any rigorous limit law results for alternative network formation methods, like the ego-centric approach. In this paper, we investigate the properties of network sampling in a model based framework and focus on the prediction of two different types of population summary statistics: subgraph counts and the clustering coefficient. We explore the effects of both induced and ego-centric network formation. We allow for sparsity in the population network and also introduce a
sparse
Bernoulli node sampling scheme to study the effects of sampling under
resource constraints
. We obtain different Gaussian and Poisson based limit laws for estimated subgraph counts and clustering coefficient, allowing for
sparsity
in both the model and the Bernoulli node sampling mechanism.

Consider an undirected population network on
N
nodes. We assume that the population network is generated from a
K
-class Stochastic Block Model (SBM) (cf.
Holland et al. (1983)
), where
K(\geq 2)
denotes the number of classes in the SBM. We focus on the SBM framework primarily due to its simplicity and flexibility and because it is widely used in applications (cf.
Lee and Wilkinson (2019)
,
Abbe (2018)
,
Airoldi et al. (2013)
). It is assumed that every node in the population network has an unique and fixed (non-stochastic) class label and the edge probability between any two nodes depends only on their respective class labels. The population network is allowed to be dense or sparse and the edge probabilities in the SBM are allowed to remain fixed or decay to zero, as the size of the population network
N
increases. Let
\pi_{N,r,s}
denote the edge probability between two nodes in the population network, one of them having class label
r
, and another having class label
s
, where
r,s\in\{1,\ldots,K\}
. We allow
\pi_{N,r,s}
to depend on
N
, and we assume that there exists a
model sparsity level

\beta\geq 0
, such that

\displaystyle N^{\beta}\pi_{N,r,s}\rightarrow c_{r,s},\hskip 10.00002pt\text{as $N\rightarrow\infty$, for some $c_{r,s}\in[0,\infty)$, for all $r,s\in\{1,\ldots,K\}$,}

(1.1)

where at least one of the
c_{r,s}
values is assumed to be positive. If
\beta=0
, then are some
\pi_{N,r,s}
values that do not decay to zero and the population network will be dense. If
\beta>0
, then
\pi_{N,r,s}\rightarrow 0
, for all
r,s\in\{1,\ldots,K\}
, as
N\rightarrow\infty
, and the population network becomes sparse. A higher value of
\beta
implies higher levels of sparsity. A Bernoulli node sampling scheme is used for selecting nodes from this population network. The node sampling probability
p_{N}
is allowed to depend on
N
and we set

\displaystyle p_{N}=\frac{c}{N^{\alpha}},\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for some $\alpha\in[0,1]$, $c>0$, and for all $N\geq 1$.}

(1.2)

The quantity
\alpha
is called the
sampling sparsity level
. If
\alpha=0
, we call the sampling
dense
; if
\alpha\in(0,1)
, the sampling is called
intermediately sparse
; and if
\alpha=1
, the sampling is called
extremely sparse
. If
\alpha=0
, then
p_{N}
remains fixed (and bounded away from
0
and
1
) and the expected number of sampled nodes is of the same order as
N
. If
\alpha\in(0,1]
, then
p_{N}
decays to zero as
N\rightarrow\infty
. In the intermediately sparse case, the expected number of sampled nodes increases with
N
, but at a much slower rate than
N
. In the extremely sparse case, the expected number of sampled nodes remains constant even as
N\rightarrow\infty
. To the best of our knowledge, this sort of
sparse node sampling
framework has not been explored in the existing literature. This framework is introduced to quantify the effects of sampling very few nodes, compared to population size
N
(cf.
Lee et al. (2006)
,
Xu et al. (2017)
,
Luo et al. (2015)
,
Bingöl et al. (2018)
), and how this affects statistical inference when the underlying population network is sparse or dense. This type of situation can arise even in cases where
N
is not very large but there are cost, storage, or access constraints on data collection or in situations where
N
is extremely large and the sample size cannot be scaled up at the same rate.

Following the initial sampling of nodes, we explore two different approaches for creating the sampled network: (a) induced approach, where the edge status between two population nodes is observed only if both nodes are sampled, and (b) ego-centric approach, where the edge status is observed if at least one of the two nodes is sampled. Induced and ego-centric network formation schemes have been widely studied in the existing literature (see
Kolaczyk (2009)
,
Bhattacharya et al. (2022)
,
Handcock and Gile (2010)
,
Hsieh et al. (2024)
,
Chandrasekhar and Lewis (2016)
). The node sampling scheme and the SBM are assumed to be
independent
. In order to describe our main contributions, we provide some initial definitions.

Throughout this article, we focus on a fixed, simple, undirected, and connected target subgraph
H
, having
R(\geq 2)
vertices and
T
edges. For any
t\in\{2,\ldots,R\}
we define
m(t:H)
as the maximum number of edges in
any

t
vertex induced subgraph of
H
(see (
3.2
)), and write
m(H)=\displaystyle\max_{2\leq t\leq R}\displaystyle m(t:H)/t
, as the
maximum edge density
of
H
and its subgraphs (see (
3.3
)). We define
m(1:H)=0
, for any choice of
H
. We say
H
is
strictly balanced
if
m(t:H)/t
is uniquely maximized at
t=R
(see p. 64 of
Janson et al. (2000)
). A collection of nodes
C
of
H
is called a
vertex cover of
H
, if an endpoint of
any
edge in
H
is contained in
C
. Let
\tau(H)
denote the
size of the minimum vertex cover of
H
(see (
3.4
)). Let
\mathbb{K}_{1,R-1}
,
\mathbb{K}_{R}
and
\mathbb{L}_{R}
, respectively, denote a star graph, a complete graph and a line graph on
R
vertices. For any two random variables
X
and
Y
,
d_{1}(X,Y)
denotes a distance measure between
X
and
Y
(cf. (
3.8
)) based on the class of all bounded maps with bounded first derivative, such that convergence in the
d_{1}
distance implies convergence in distribution. Let
S_{N}(H)
,
\widehat{S}_{N,1}(H)
and
\widehat{S}_{N,2}(H)
(see (
3.5
)) denote the subgraph count (number of copies) of
H
in the population network, the estimate obtained from the network sample constructed by induced subgraph formation, and the estimate obtained from the network sample constructed by ego-centric subgraph formation, respectively. The centered and scaled sample based subgraph counts
T_{N,1}(H)
(induced case) and
T_{N,2}(H)
(ego-centric case) are defined as

\left.\begin{aligned} T_{N,l}(H)&=\frac{\widehat{S}_{N,l}(H)-f_{l}(p_{N}:H)\cdot S_{N}(H)}{\sigma_{N,l}(H)},\quad\text{where,}\\
\sigma^{2}_{N,l}(H)&=\mathbf{Var}\left(\widehat{S}_{N,l}(H)-f_{l}(p_{N}:H)\cdot S_{N}(H)\right),\end{aligned}\hskip 20.00003pt\right\}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for $l=1,2$.}

(1.3)

The variance term
\sigma^{2}_{N,l}(H)
in (
1.3
) is found jointly with respect to the model (SBM) and the node sampling design, taking into account the stochasticity from both the population network model and the node sampling scheme. The terms
f_{l}(p_{N}:H)
,
l=1,2
, are defined in (
3.6
), and are used for correct centering and to account for the effects of sampling.

1.1
Overview of Gaussian approximation results for estimated subgraph counts

One of the primary contributions of this article is to provide quantitative bounds on the speed of normal approximation for
T_{N,l}(H)
, for
l=1,2
, for suitable choices of
H
, and describe sufficient conditions on the model and sampling sparsity levels
\beta
and
\alpha
, which will ensure asymptotic normality. Let
Z\sim N(0,1)
. Suppose
\beta
and
\alpha
denote the underlying model and sampling sparsity levels. Define

\displaystyle\Delta_{N,l}(H;\alpha,\beta)\equiv d_{1}\left(T_{N,l}(H),Z\right),\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for any $\alpha,\beta\geq 0$, and $l=1,2$ (cf. \eqref{TN-def-12}),}

(1.4)

where both
\alpha
and
\beta
are included in the definition of
\Delta_{N,l}(H;\alpha,\beta)
to account for the effects of the underlying sampling and model sparsity levels. In Theorem
3.1
we show that for any target subgraph
H
that satisfies certain assumptions, and for any
\alpha,\beta\geq 0
, the
d_{1}
distance
\Delta_{N,l}(H;\alpha,\beta)
(cf. (
1.4
)) satisfies the following upper bound,

\left.\begin{aligned} &\Delta_{N,l}(H;\alpha,\beta)=O\left(N^{~\displaystyle\left\{g_{l,\alpha,\beta,H}\left(t_{l}(\alpha,\beta;H)\right)/2\right\}\displaystyle}\right),\quad\text{for $l=1,2$, as $N\rightarrow\infty$, where}\\
&t_{l}(\alpha,\beta;H)\in\operatorname{argmax}\left\{g_{l,\alpha,\beta,H}(t):t=1,\ldots,R\right\},\quad\text{for $l=1,2$, with}\\
&\left.\begin{aligned} &g_{1,\alpha,\beta,H}(t)=\displaystyle{}-t+t\cdot\alpha+m(t:H)\cdot\beta\displaystyle,\quad\text{and}\\
&g_{2,\alpha,\beta,H}(t)=\displaystyle{}-t+\min\{\tau(H),t\}\cdot\alpha+m(t:H)\cdot\beta,\displaystyle\end{aligned}\quad\right\}\ \text{for $t=1,\ldots,R$.}\end{aligned}\right\}

(1.5)

The bound in (
1.5
) shows that the
d_{1}
distance between
T_{N,l}(H)
and
Z
is controlled by the model and the sampling sparsity levels
\beta
and
\alpha
, and this also depends on two different features of the target subgraph: (i) the collection of all edge densities
\{m(t:H)/t:t\in\{1,\ldots,R\}\}
, for both induced and ego-centric cases; (ii) the minimum vertex cover size
\tau(H)
, for the ego-centric case. To obtain an asymptotic normality result, one has to show that the bound on
\Delta_{N,l}(H;\alpha,\beta)
converges to zero. This can be achieved if the sparsity levels
\beta
and
\alpha
are restricted within a threshold, and this threshold will depend on the target subgraph
H
. In the induced case (
l=1
), for
any
simple, connected, and undirected target subgraph
H
, we define the following region of model and sampling sparsity levels,

\displaystyle C_{1}(H)

\displaystyle=\left\{(\alpha,\beta):\displaystyle\alpha+\frac{\beta}{\frac{t}{m(t:H)}}<1\displaystyle,~\text{for $t=2,\ldots,R$},~\alpha\in[0,1),\beta\geq 0\right\}.

(1.6)

Using the definitions provided in (
1.5
), we can write
C_{1}(H)=\{(\alpha,\beta):g_{1,\alpha,\beta,H}(t_{1}(\alpha,\beta;H))<0,\alpha\in[0,1),\beta\geq 0\}
. Clearly, the above description shows that if
(\alpha,\beta)\in C_{1}(H)
, then
\Delta_{N,1}(H;\alpha,\beta)\rightarrow 0
, as
N\rightarrow\infty
, thus ensuring the asymptotic normality of
T_{N,1}(H)
.

In the ego-centric case, the allowable choices of
H
depend on the sampling sparsity level
\alpha
. If the sampling is sparse,
i.e.
,
\alpha\in(0,1)
, in addition to the features described above in the induced case, we require
H
to satisfy a specific assumption (see Assumption
(A.5)
in Section
3
). Common target subgraphs such as the complete graph or the star graph, as well as numerous other graphs, satisfy this assumption. If the sampling is dense (
\alpha=0
), then
H
does not need to satisfy this assumption. In fact, in the ego-centric case (
l=2
), the bound on
\Delta_{N,2}(H:\alpha,\beta)
in (
1.5
) can be obtained at an
\alpha>0
, if
H
satisfies this assumption. In the ego-centric case, for a given choice of
H
, we define the following region of model and sampling sparsity levels
\beta
and
\alpha
,

\displaystyle C_{2}(H)

\displaystyle=\left\{(\alpha,\beta):~\displaystyle\frac{\alpha}{\frac{t}{\min\{t,\tau(H)\}}}+\frac{\beta}{\frac{t}{m(t:H)}}<1\displaystyle,~\text{for $t=2,\ldots,R$, $\alpha\in[0,1)$, $\beta\geq 0$}\right\}.

(1.7)

Rewriting the constraints within
C_{2}(H)
in terms of
g_{2,\alpha,\beta,H}(\cdot)
and
t_{2}(\alpha,\beta;H)
(see (
1.5
)), we can show that if
(\alpha,\beta)\in C_{2}(H)
, then
T_{N,2}(H)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\rightarrow}}N(0,1)
.

This asymptotic normality result is obtained under a joint model and design based framework. To the best of our knowledge, the asymptotic distribution of ego-centric sampling based estimates have not been studied in the existing literature under any type of framework (design or model based), and the same has not been done for the induced case in a model based setting. If the sampling is extremely sparse (
\alpha=1
), a finite amount of data is observed in the induced case and it can be shown that
T_{N,1}(H)
will not converge in distribution. In the ego-centric case, at
\alpha=1
, our results are limited to the case of
H=\mathbb{K}_{1,R-1}
(star graph), where we show that a Gaussian limit law does not arise.

The bound in (
1.5
) provides deep insight about the quality of normal approximation and how it is affected by the levels of model and sampling sparsity, the mechanism of network formation after node sampling, and the edge structure of the target subgraph. The speed of normal approximation for
T_{N,l}(H)
depends on the magnitude of the term
g_{l,\alpha,\beta,H}(t_{l}(\alpha,\beta;H))
in the exponent of
N
(cf. (
1.5
)). From the definition of
g_{l,\alpha,\beta,H}(\cdot)
, it follows that the influence of the sparsity levels
\beta
and
\alpha
is transmitted through the edge structure of
H
. For example, in the ego-centric case, an increase in the sampling sparsity level
\alpha
contributes at most
\tau(H)\cdot\alpha
to the growth of
g_{2,\alpha,\beta,H}(t)
, even as
t
increases beyond
\tau(H)
. The same is not true in the induced case. This explains how in the case of a star graph
H=\mathbb{K}_{1,R-1}
, with
\tau(H)=1
, the ego-centric approach can handle relatively high levels of model sparsity
\beta
and provide a Gaussian limit law for
T_{N,2}(\mathbb{K}_{1,R-1})
, even if the sampling sparsity level
\alpha
is high. Similarly, the impact of increasing the model sparsity level
\beta
is regulated by the values of the edge densities
\{m(t:H)/t:t=1,\ldots,R\}
.

Each
C_{l}(H)
,
l=1,2
(cf. (
1.6
) and (
1.7
)), can be further split into
sub-regions
, based on the value of the maxima
t_{l}(\alpha,\beta;H)
(see (
1.5
)). Define

\displaystyle C_{l,j}(H)=\left\{(\alpha,\beta)\in C_{l}(H):t_{l}(\alpha,\beta;H)=j\right\},\hskip 10.00002pt\text{$j=1,\ldots,R$, $l=1,2$.}

(1.8)

For some choices of
j
,
C_{l,j}(H)
can be empty, but
\{C_{l,j}:j=1,\ldots,R\}
forms a partition of
C_{l}(H)
. The amount of influence exerted by the model and sampling sparsity levels
\beta
and
\alpha
and the speed of normal approximation in (
1.5
) changes sharply when
(\alpha,\beta)
move over different sub-regions. Similarly, the growth rate of the variance term in (
1.3
) also changes over these sub-regions. To explain this clearly and to illustrate the utility of the bound in (
1.5
), we consider the specific example of
H=\mathbb{K}_{3}
(triangle).

(a) Induced case:
C_{1}(\mathbb{K}_{3})
and its sub-regions.

(b) Ego-centric case:
C_{2}(\mathbb{K}_{3})
and its sub-regions.

Figure 1
:
Sparsity levels and limit laws for estimated triangle counts:

T_{N,1}(\mathbb{K}_{3})
and
T_{N,2}(\mathbb{K}_{3})
have a Gaussian limit on
C_{1}(\mathbb{K}_{3})
and
C_{2}(\mathbb{K}_{3})
(yellow shaded regions), with different speeds of normal approximation within each sub-region (cf. (
1.9
) and (
1.10
)). Similar types of Poisson based limit laws arise at
(\alpha,\beta)=(0,R/T)=(0,1)
(marked by filled black dot on both figures) for both
T_{N,l}(\mathbb{K}_{3})
,
l=1,2
, see Theorem
3.4
(a). A different type of Poisson based limit law arises on the upper boundaries of
C_{1,3}(\mathbb{K}_{3})
and
C_{2,3}(\mathbb{K}_{3})
(marked by solid red line), see Theorem
3.4
(b). The limiting behavior of
T_{N,2}(\mathbb{K}_{3})
remains unknown on the triangular region
U
, on and beyond the boundary of the sub-region
C_{2,2}(\mathbb{K}_{3})
. The triangle graph satisfies Assumption
(A.5)
.

The yellow shaded regions in Figures
1
(a) and
1
(b) show the sets
C_{1}(\mathbb{K}_{3})
and
C_{2}(\mathbb{K}_{3})
(see (
1.6
) and (
1.7
)) and their non-empty sub-regions:
C_{1,1}(\mathbb{K}_{3})
and
C_{1,3}(\mathbb{K}_{3})
, in the induced case; and
C_{2,1}(\mathbb{K}_{3})
,
C_{2,2}(\mathbb{K}_{3})
and
C_{2,3}(\mathbb{K}_{3})
, in the ego-centric case. Using the bound in (
1.5
), we can obtain the following results for
H=\mathbb{K}_{3}
,

\displaystyle\Delta_{N,1}(\mathbb{K}_{3};\alpha,\beta)=\begin{cases}O\left(N^{{}-\left(1-\alpha\right)/2}\right)&\text{if $(\alpha,\beta)\in C_{1,1}(\mathbb{K}_{3})=\left\{(\alpha,\beta):0\leq\beta<\frac{2(1-\alpha)}{3}\right\}$,}\\
O\left(N^{{}-3\left(1-\alpha-\beta\right)/2}\right)&\text{if $(\alpha,\beta)\in C_{1,3}(\mathbb{K}_{3})=\left\{(\alpha,\beta):\frac{2(1-\alpha)}{3}\leq\beta<(1-\alpha)\right\}$,}\end{cases}

(1.9)

and,

\displaystyle\Delta_{N,2}(\mathbb{K}_{3};\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}O\left(N^{{}-(1-\alpha)/2}\right)&\text{if $(\alpha,\beta)\in C_{2,1}(\mathbb{K}_{3})=\left\{(\alpha,\beta):\alpha+\beta<1,\frac{\alpha}{2}+\frac{3\beta}{2}<1\right\}$,}\\
O\left(N^{{}-(1-\alpha)+\beta/2}\right)&\text{if $(\alpha,\beta)\in C_{2,2}(\mathbb{K}_{3})=\left\{(\alpha,\beta):\alpha+\beta\geq 1,\beta<\frac{1}{2},\alpha+\frac{\beta}{2}<1\right\}$,}\\
O\left(N^{{}-3\left(1-2\alpha/3-\beta\right)/2}\right)&\text{if $(\alpha,\beta)\in C_{2,3}(\mathbb{K}_{3})=\left\{(\alpha,\beta):\frac{\alpha}{2}+\frac{3\beta}{2}>1,\beta>\frac{1}{2},\frac{2\alpha}{3}+\beta<1\right\}$.}\end{cases}

(1.10)

The bounds in (
1.9
) and (
1.10
) provide a precise understanding of the influence of the model and sampling sparsity levels
\beta
and
\alpha
, as
(\alpha,\beta)
vary over different sub-regions within
C_{l}(\mathbb{K}_{3})
,
l=1,2
. Similar results can be obtained for other choices of
H
. This information can be used by practitioners to choose the sampling sparsity level during data collection. One can show that at any common
(\alpha,\beta)\in C_{1}(H)\cap C_{2}(H)
, the ego-centric approach is always preferable, in terms of speed of convergence to normality (see Remark
2
). Remarkably, our results on Poisson approximation for
T_{N,l}(H)
,
l=1,2
, show that the thresholds on the sparsity levels
(\alpha,\beta)
, provided in
C_{1}(H)
(cf. (
1.6
)) and
C_{2}(H)
(cf. (
1.7
)) also turn out to be
necessary
for obtaining a Gaussian limit law.

Obtaining the bound on the
d_{1}
distance in (
1.5
) for an arbitrary choice of
H
is a non-trivial task, especially in the ego-centric case. The first difficulty is to analyze the dependency within the components of
T_{N,l}(H)
(cf. (
1.3
)), which arises from the joint effect of the sampling design and the network model. The strength of sampling based dependency is related to the number of common nodes while model based dependency is related to the number of common edges. There is no simple approach to relate these two sources of dependency. Secondly, we require the
exact
rate of growth of the variance
\sigma^{2}_{N,l}(H)
(cf. (
1.3
)) and to obtain this we have to identify the covariance term that has the highest contribution. This task is exceptionally difficult in the ego-centric case due to the presence of the minimum vertex cover size term. To address this issue, we have developed some novel upper and lower bounds on the minimum vertex cover size of the union of two or three arbitrary isomorphic copies of
H
. Lastly, we had to devise an approach to control the third order moments of the pivotal quantity in (
1.3
) and compare them with the growth rate of the variance term
\sigma^{2}_{N,l}(H)
.

1.2
Overview of Poisson based approximation results for estimated subgraph counts and results about the estimated clustering coefficient

As the sparsity levels
\beta
and
\alpha
increase and reach the boundaries of the sets
C_{1}(H)
and
C_{2}(H)
(see (
1.6
) and (
1.7
)), we observe sharp distributional transitions. We study the limit laws of
T_{N,l}(H)
,
l=1,2
, at these boundary values of
(\alpha,\beta)
, but focus only on those
H
, which are strictly balanced. As discussed earlier, in the ego-centric case, if
\alpha>0
, we assume that
H
satisfies Assumption
(A.5)
. Most common target graphs are strictly balanced. Theorem
3.4
provides three different types of Poisson distribution based limit laws for
T_{N,l}(H)
,
l=1,2
, depending on the values of
(\alpha,\beta)
and the choice of
H
. Our results are exhaustive in the induced case. In the ego-centric setting, we provide a partially complete description, and some open questions remain. Our findings are listed below.

(a)

When the sampling is dense (
\alpha=0
), Theorem
3.4
(a) shows that both
T_{N,1}(H)
and
T_{N,2}(H)
have similar Poisson based limit laws at the extreme model sparsity level
\beta=R/T
. These limit laws are expressible as weighted sum of two independent Poisson random variables, and they differ only in their underlying parameters. In Figures
1
(a) and
1
(b), this corresponds to the boundary point
(\alpha,\beta)=(0,1)
(marked by a solid black dot).

(b)

If the sampling is intermediately sparse (
\alpha\in(0,1)
), a second type of Poisson based limit law is obtained for
T_{N,1}(H)
, in the boundary region of
C_{1}(H)
(see Theorem
3.4
(b)), for all choices of
\alpha\in(0,1)
. In the ego-centric case, a similar type of Poisson based limit law (with different underlying parameters) is obtained for
T_{N,2}(H)
in the boundary region of
C_{2}(H)
, when
\tau(H)=1
,
i.e.
, when
H=\mathbb{K}_{1,R-1}
(star graph).

In case of
\tau(H)>1
, we obtain similar results for
T_{N,2}(H)
at the boundary of
C_{2}(H)
,
but
our results are restricted and are only available for the range
\alpha\in(0,\alpha^{\star}_{H})
, where
\alpha^{\star}_{H}\in(0,1)
(cf. (
3.16
)) and depends on
H
. See Theorem
3.4
(b) for more details. Thus, for
\tau(H)>1
, the limit law of
T_{N,2}(H)
at certain boundary values of
C_{2}(H)
remains unknown when
\alpha\in[\alpha^{\star}_{H},1)
.

(c)

In the case of extremely sparse sampling (
\alpha=1
), too few sampled data points are available to obtain a proper limit law for
T_{N,1}(H)
in the induced case. In the ego-centric case, we were able to obtain a Poisson based limit law for
T_{N,2}(H)
only in the case of
\tau(H)=1
,
i.e.
, when
H=\mathbb{K}_{1,R-1}
is a star graph (see Theorem
3.4
(c)). If
\tau(H)>1
, then the limit law of
T_{N,2}(H)
remains unknown in the case of
\alpha=1
.

To find these three types of limit laws, different techniques were used. This includes the application of multivariate size biased Poisson approximation results (cf.
Nualart and Zhang (2025)
) in the context of network sampling to handle the dense sampling case and the use of total variation bounds for Poisson approximation (see Section
3.1
for further details) to handle the intermediately sparse case. The emergence of these Poisson-based limit laws shows that the upper thresholds on the sparsity levels
(\alpha,\beta)\in C_{l}(H)
,
l=1,2
, which ensure a Gaussian limit law for
T_{N,l}(H)
,
l=1,2
, are also
necessary
. An overview of the different criteria and restrictions that were used to derive the Gaussian and Poisson based limit laws is provided in Table
. The conditions are classified according to the underlying sampling sparsity level.

\ctable

T_{N,l}(H)
,
l=1,2
, are obtained at different sampling sparsity regimes.,

and

. Require

\tau(H)=1
for
\beta\in[0,1)
. Limit laws are unknown when
\tau(H)>1
. See Theorem
3.4
(c).

As an application of the limit law results for subgraph counts we derive the asymptotic distribution of the sample based clustering coefficient statistic, in both induced and ego-centric cases (see Theorems
3.5
and
3.6
). The clustering coefficient is an important measure of network cohesion (see
Kolaczyk (2009)
). Various researchers have focused on the estimation of the clustering coefficient through network sampling (cf.
Bliss et al. (2014)
,
Lee et al. (2006)
,
Illenberger and Flötteröd (2012)
). To the best our knowledge, the asymptotic distribution of the appropriately centered sample based clustering coefficient, which is a function of both the sample based and population based wedge and triangle counts, has not been studied in the existing literature under any sort of framework or under any type of sampling scheme. Our results show that for both induced and ego-centric cases, the estimated clustering coefficient has a Gaussian limit law if the sparsity levels remain below the thresholds defined by
C_{l}(\mathbb{K}_{3})
,
l=1,2
(cf. (
1.6
), (
1.7
)). In the boundary regions of
C_{l}(\mathbb{K}_{3})
, we obtain various Poisson-based limit laws. The Gaussian limit law is driven by the joint effect of the wedge (
\mathbb{K}_{1,2}
) and triangle counts, if
(\alpha,\beta)\in C_{1,1}(\mathbb{K}_{3})
(induced case) and if
(\alpha,\beta)\in C_{2,1}(\mathbb{K}_{3})
(ego-centric case). If
(\alpha,\beta)
move beyond these sub-regions, the Gaussian limit law is influenced only by the triangle count. The asymptotic variance expressions are complicated, and the details are provided in Section
3.2
.

Using these limit laws, we obtain asymptotic prediction intervals for the population subgraph counts and the clustering coefficient. A simulation study explores the coverage accuracy of these prediction intervals at various levels of model sparsity and sampling sparsity, including extreme and intermediate levels. Our simulations strongly support our theoretical results. One of the primary challenges is to compute the variance term
\sigma^{2}_{N,l}(H)
(cf. (
1.3
)), for any arbitrary choice of
H
. In Remark
9
we provide detailed expressions of the variance
\sigma^{2}_{N,l}(H)
, for
l=1,2
, for
any
choice of
H
. The code for computing this complicated variance expression is also included in the

github page
, along with all other codes and data used in this article. We also successfully use our proposed methodology to the
2004 US political blog network
data from
Adamic and Glance (2005)
, which is known to have a block structure (cf.
Le and Levina (2022)
,
Amini et al. (2013)
).

The remainder of the article is organized as follows. The sparse SBM and the sparse node sampling scheme are introduced in Section
2
, where we provide reasons for using the joint model and design based inferential approach. In Section
3
, we provide the main theoretical results of this article. This includes CLT’s for
T_{N,l}(H)
,
l=1,2
, along with the decay rates for the
d_{1}
distance, a description of the Poisson-based limit laws at the boundary of
C_{l}(H)
,
l=1,2
, and limit laws for the estimated clustering coefficient in both induced and ego-centric cases. A detailed simulation study and real data analysis is provided in Section
4
. The Appendix contains proofs of main results, supplementary lemmas, description of essential results from other sources, and formulas for
\sigma^{2}_{N,l}(H)
(cf. (
1.3
)) for some common target subgraphs.

2
The model for the population network and the node sampling scheme

Consider a population network on
N
nodes, labeled
1,\ldots,N
, and a symmetric binary relation among pairs of nodes. The terms
vertex
and
node
will be used interchangeably. We will represent this population network as an undirected, simple graph
G_{N}
, with a vertex set
V(G_{N})=\{1,\ldots,N\}=[N]
and an edge set
E(G_{N})
. An edge exists (does not exist) between two nodes if the binary relation exists (does not exist). Let
{[V(G_{N})]}^{2}
denote the set of all two element subsets of
V(G_{N})
. Then
E(G_{N})\subseteq{[V(G_{N})]}^{2}
, is a collection of two element subsets of
V(G_{N})
(cf.
Diestel (2018)
). An edge between nodes
i
and
j
will be represented by the two element set
\{i,j\}
. The network
G_{N}
can be described in terms of a
N\times N
symmetric adjacency matrix
\mathbf{Y}_{N}
, where

\displaystyle\mathbf{Y}_{N}=((Y_{N,i,j}:1\leq i,j\leq N)),\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptY_{N,i,j}=Y_{N,j,i}=\begin{cases}1&\text{if $\{i,j\}\in E(G_{N})$,}\\
0&\text{if $\{i,j\}\not\in E(G_{N})$.}\end{cases}

(2.1)

We assume that the network does not contain multiple edges and self-edges,
i.e.
,
Y_{N,i,i}=0
, for each
i\in[N]
.

In this article we use a model based approach and assume that the population network is stochastically generated from a
sparse
Stochastic Block Model (SBM). The SBM was proposed by
Holland et al. (1983)
and it assumes that nodes in the population network are split into
K
disjoint classes and edge probabilities depend on the class label of each node. We will assume
K\geq 2
and
K
remains fixed even as
N
increases. For each
i\in[N]
, we assume that the
i
-th node in the population has an unique and non-stochastic class label
\alpha_{i}\in[K]=\{1,\ldots,K\}
, and
\alpha_{i}
does not depend on
N
. Stochastic block models with fixed class labels have been widely used (see
Abbe (2018)
,
Rohe et al. (2011)
,
Lei and Rinaldo (2015)
,
Celisse et al. (2012)
,
Anderson et al. (1992)
), especially in the community detection literature. We define a sequence of
K\times K
symmetric matrices of class-wise edge probabilities

\bm{\Pi}_{N}=((\pi_{N,i,j},1\leq i,j\leq K)),\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for all $N\geq 1$,}

(2.2)

where
\pi_{N,i,j}
denotes the probability of an edge between a node with class label
i
and another node with class label
j
, in the population network
G_{N}
. We assume that the probabilities
\pi_{N,i,j}
can depend on the size (
N
) of population network. We also assume that the edges
\{Y_{N,i,j}:1\leq i<j\leq N\}
are independently generated and

\displaystyle Y_{N,i,j}~{\sim}\text{ Bernoulli }(\pi_{N,\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j}})\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for all $1\leq i<j\leq N$ and for every $N\geq 1$.}

(2.3)

If
K=1
, the SBM reduces to the Erdős-Rényi model (cf.
Erdős and Rényi (1960)
). For ease of reference, we summarize the model description given above in the form of the following assumption.

(A.1)

We assume that the population network
G_{N}
is stochastically generated by a
K
-class Stochastic Block Model with edge probability matrix
\bm{\Pi}_{N}
(cf. (
2.2
)), where
K(\geq 2)
is fixed and does not depend on
N
. Each node in
G_{N}
has a fixed and non-stochastic class label
\alpha_{i}\in[K]
, which does not depend on
N
. We also assume that the edges in
G_{N}
are independently generated as per (
2.3
).

For simplicity of notation, we will drop the subscript
N
and write
Y_{N,i,j}=Y_{i,j}
, for all
1\leq i,j\leq N
and all
N\geq 1
. Write

\displaystyle N_{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbf{1}(\alpha_{i}=k),\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\lambda_{N,k}=\frac{N_{k}}{N},\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for all $k\in[K]$ and $N\geq 1$.}

(2.4)

These denote the count and the proportion of nodes in
G_{N}
, which have class label
k
. We assume the following:

(A.2)

There exists a collection of constants
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{K}\in(0,1)
, which satisfy the constraint
\sum_{k=1}^{K}\lambda_{k}=1
, and the class size proportions
\lambda_{N,k}\rightarrow\lambda_{k}
, as
N\rightarrow\infty
, for each
k\in[K]
.

Assumption
(A.2)
describes the growth rate of
N_{k}
and assumes that the size of each class in the population grows linearly with
N
, as
N\rightarrow\infty
. It ensures that none of the
K
classes in the population are negligible. If
\lambda_{k_{0}}=0
, for some
k_{0}\in[K]
, then we can drop that class and work with the remaining
(K-1)
classes.

We focus on the SBM as it is one of the simplest network models which preserves edge independence, and yet it is flexible to approximate more complex network models (cf.
Airoldi et al. (2013)
,
Olhede and Wolfe (2014)
,
Matias and Robin (2014)
). Edge independence has been the building block of other types of network models (cf.
Sischka (2023)
,
Athreya et al. (2018)
). Additionally, SBM’s are widely used in applications (cf.
Lee and Wilkinson (2019)
,
Nicola et al. (2022)
,
Abbe (2018)
).
Lee and Wilkinson (2019)
provide a detailed review about stochastic block models, its variations and applications. We now introduce
sparsity
into the SBM through the following assumption.

(A.3)

There exists a constant
\beta\geq 0
, and a
K\times K
symmetric matrix
\mathbf{C}=((c_{i,j}:1\leq i,j\leq K))
, such that the edge probability matrix
\bm{\Pi}_{N}
defined in (
2.2
) satisfies

\left.\begin{aligned} &\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}N^{\beta}\cdot\bm{\Pi}_{N}=\mathbf{C},\quad\text{where,}\\
&c_{i,j}\in[0,\infty),\quad\text{for all $1\leq i,j\leq K$,}\quad\text{and}\quad\max\{c_{i,i}:1\leq i\leq K\}\in(0,\infty).\end{aligned}\right\}

(2.5)

To avoid trivialities we assume that there exists at least two unique entries in
\mathbf{C}
. If
\beta=0
, we assume
\bm{\Pi}_{N}=\bm{\Pi}
for all
N\geq 1
, and
\mathbf{C}=\bm{\Pi}
, where
\bm{\Pi}
is a fixed edge probability matrix.

Assumption
(A.3)
implies that if
\beta=0
, then some edge probabilities in
\bm{\Pi}
remain positive and as a consequence we obtain a dense SBM through (
2.3
). If
\beta>0
, then all edge probabilities in
\bm{\Pi}_{N}
decay to zero as
N\rightarrow\infty
, resulting in a sparse population network. The quantity
\beta
is called the model sparsity level and it is introduced to quantify the amount of sparsity in the network model. Several researchers have constructed sparse network models in this manner by introducing such an extraneous sparsity controlling parameter (cf.
Bickel et al. (2011)
,
Lei and Rinaldo (2015)
,
Bhattacharyya and Bickel (2015)
,
Agterberg and Cape (2025)
,
Stewart and Schweinberger (2025)
,
Coulson et al. (2016)
,
Matsushita and Otsu (2024)
). Similar approaches have been extensively used to model sparse Erdős-Rényi random graphs (see
Janson et al. (2000)
). When
\beta>0
and
c_{i,j}=0
, then
\pi_{N,i,j}\ll N^{-\beta}
. If
c_{i,j}>0
, then
\pi_{N,i,j}
has the same rate as
N^{-\beta}
. As per Assumption
(A.3)
, some entries of
\mathbf{C}
remain positive and all entries of
\mathbf{C}
are finite. Thus, there cannot exist two different choices of
\beta
which satisfy (
2.5
). We require
\max_{i\in[K]}c_{i,i}>0
, to ensure that all asymptotic variance terms obtained in our theoretical results remain positive. This is a very mild requirement.

Now we describe the sparse Bernoulli node sampling scheme and the induced and ego-centric network formation mechanisms. We make the following assumption about the Bernoulli node sampling scheme.

(A.4)

Each of the
N
nodes in the population network
G_{N}
is sampled independently with probability

\displaystyle p_{N}=\frac{c}{N^{\alpha}},\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for some $\alpha\in[0,1]$ and some $c>0$, for all $N\geq 1$.}

(2.6)

If
\alpha=0
, we assume
p_{N}=c
, and
c\in(0,1)
.

If
\alpha=0
, the node sampling scheme is called
dense
, otherwise we call it
sparse
. We consider three levels of sampling sparsity:
\alpha=0
(dense),
\alpha\in(0,1)
(intermediate) and
\alpha=1
(extreme).
Obviously, a dense sampling scheme with
\alpha=0
is always preferable if adequate resources are available. However, if there are resource constraints, it can limit the number of nodes that can be sampled (cf.
Leskovec and Faloutsos (2006)
,
Lee et al. (2006)
,
Luo et al. (2015)
). For example, if
N
is extremely large, it may be sensible to use a probability of node selection that depends on the size of the population
N
. The sparse node sampling framework is introduced to understand the effects of network sampling under resource constraints. We quantify the effects of sparse and dense node sampling and how their effects are related to the structure of the target graph, the sparsity level of the population model, and the network formation mechanism. In many situations, accurate inferential conclusions can be obtained even when the model or the sampling, or both, are sparse. To the best of our knowledge, this type of sparse node sampling framework has not been studied in the existing literature on network sampling, especially in a model based setup. The sparse sampling approach provides a more finer understanding of the node sampling methodology and its effects. We define the node inclusion indicators

\displaystyle W_{N,i}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}1&\text{if the $i$-th node is selected,}\\
0&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for all $i\in[N]$, and write}

(2.7)

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}_{N}

\displaystyle={(W_{N,1},\ldots,W_{N,N})}^{T},\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for each $N\geq 1$.}

Note,
\{W_{N,i}:i\in[N]\}
are independent and identically distributed (i.i.d.) Bernoulli
(p_{N})
random variables (see Assumption
(A.4)
) for all
N\geq 1
. Define the maps,

\displaystyle h_{1},h_{2}:\{0,1\}\times\{0,1\}\mapsto\{0,1\},\hskip 10.00002pt\text{where,}\hskip 10.00002pth_{1}(x,y)=x\cdot y,\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pth_{2}(x,y)=\max\{x,y\}.

(2.8)

Once the nodes are sampled, we consider two distinct network formation methods based on the sampled nodes.

(a)

Induced subgraph formation: the
(i,j)^{th}
entry of
\mathbf{Y}_{N}
is observed in the induced subgraph formation approach if both nodes
i
and
j
are sampled,
i.e.
,
h_{1}(W_{N,i},W_{N,j})=W_{N,i}W_{N,j}=1
.

(b)

Ego-centric subgraph formation: the
(i,j)^{th}
entry of
\mathbf{Y}_{N}
is observed in the ego-centric subgraph formation approach if either of the nodes
i
and
j
is sampled,
i.e.
,
h_{2}(W_{N,i},W_{N,j})=\max\{W_{N,i},W_{N,j}\}=1
.

Node sampling schemes are very common (cf.
Kolaczyk (2009)
) and both induced and ego-centric sampling schemes were considered in
Handcock and Gile (2010)
,
Hsieh et al. (2024)
and
Chandrasekhar and Lewis (2016)
, while
Bhattacharya et al. (2022)
only considered induced subgraph formation. Note,
\mathbf{W}_{N}
(cf. (
2.7
)) is independent of the population adjancency matrix
\mathbf{Y}_{N}
.

As pointed out by
Handcock and Gile (2010)
, network sampling based inference can be carried out using a design based (cf.
Frank (2005)
,
Frank (1978a)
) or a model based approach (cf.
Thompson and Frank (2000)
,
Vincent and Thompson (2021)
,
Snijders (1992)
,
Gile and Handcock (2015)
,
Handcock and Gile (2010)
). In our setting, the node sampling scheme is independent of the population network model. We need to decide whether the effect of the sampling design should be included in our inferential framework, along with population model, when our primary objective is to predict population network summary statistics. The SBM formulation described above (see Assumption
(A.1)
) uses
non-stochastic
and fixed class labels
\{\alpha_{i}:i\in[N]\}
. This implies, the distributions of
Y_{i,j}
,
1\leq i<j\leq N
, are not identical and the nodes in
G_{N}
are
not
homogeneous in terms of their edge forming characteristics. In fact, when the class labels are fixed, the SBM is no longer
vertex exchangeable
(see
Crane (2018)
,
Sischka (2023)
) and the stochastic block model formulation used in Assumption
(A.1)
cannot be cast in a graphon based framework. This implies, ignoring the design effect by conditioning on
\mathbf{W}_{N}
(see (
2.7
)) and focusing on a
pre-selected
set of nodes would create bias. This type of bias can be removed if we include the effects of the sampling design (along with the population model) in our inferential framework. Joint design and model based approaches are frequently used in survey sampling (see Chapter 39 of
Pfeffermann (2000)
,
Rubin-Bleuer and Schiopu Kratina (2005)
,
Boistard et al. (2017)
). Recall the definition of
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
from (
2.8
) and write

\displaystyle{I}_{l,N}=\{(i,j)\in[N]^{2}:h_{l}(W_{N,i},W_{N,j})=1\}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\mathcal{Y}_{l,N}=\{Y_{U,V}:(U,V)\in I_{l,N}\},\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for $l=1,2$.}

(2.9)

Here,
I_{l,N}
and
\mathcal{Y}_{l,N}
denote the collection of indices
(i,j)
and the corresponding
Y_{i,j}
values, which have been observed through the induced (
l=1
) and the ego-centric (
l=2
) approaches. Note, for any
(U,V)\in I_{l,N}
,
\mathbf{P}(Y_{U,V}=1)\neq\mathbf{P}(Y_{i,j}=1)
, for any
(i,j)\in{[N]}^{2}
. Also, if
(U,V_{1}),(U,V_{2})\in I_{l,N}
, then
Y_{U,V_{1}}
and
Y_{U,V_{2}}
are dependent, even though the
Y_{i,j}
’s in the population remain independent (see (
2.3
)). Thus, once the effect of sampling design is included, the original distribution of the
Y_{i,j}
’s is distorted and the observed data no longer remain independent. This also implies that asymptotic results developed in a design based framework (with a non-stochastic population adjacency matrix) can not be carried over trivially to a joint model and design based framework.

3
Theoretical results

The main theoretical results of this paper are presented in this section. Firstly, we introduce some required notation and definitions. For any positive integer
m
, we write
[m]=\{1,\ldots,m\}
to denote the set of the first
m
positive integers. For any two sequences of positive real numbers
\{a_{N}:N\geq 1\}
and
\{b_{N}:N\geq 1\}
, we write
a_{N}\asymp b_{N}
, if
a_{N}/b_{N}\rightarrow 1
, as
N\rightarrow\infty
. Similarly, we write
a_{N}\sim b_{N}
, if there exist constants
0<m_{1}<m_{2}<\infty
, and an integer
N_{0}\geq 1
, such that
m_{1}<a_{N}/b_{N}<m_{2}
, for all
N\geq N_{0}
. We will always assume that the target subgraph
H
is a fixed, simple, undirected and connected graph, with vertex set
V(H)=[R]
and edge set
E(H)\subseteq{[V(H)]}^{2}
. Thus, we will not consider target graphs
H
with isolated nodes. We assume
R(\geq 2)
is a fixed positive integer and
N\geq R
. The population network
G_{N}
is represented as a graph with adjacency matrix
\mathbf{Y}_{N}=((Y_{i,j}:1\leq i,j\leq N))
(cf. (
2.1
)). Recall that
V(G_{N})=[N]
. Define the following collections of
R
dimensional vectors,

\displaystyle{\displaystyle[N]}^{R}=\left\{\mathbf{s}={\left(s_{1},\ldots,s_{R}\right)}^{T}:s_{i}\in[N],\ \text{for all $i\in[R]$}\right\},\hskip 10.00002pt{[N]}_{R}=\left\{\mathbf{s}\in{[N]}^{R}:\text{$s_{1},\ldots,s_{R}$ are distinct}\right\},\ \text{and}

(3.1)

\displaystyle{\displaystyle[N]}_{<,R}=\left\{\mathbf{s}\in{[N]}_{R}:s_{1}<s_{2}<\cdots<s_{R}\right\}.

For any
\mathbf{u}={(u_{1},\ldots,u_{m})}^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
, we write
A(\mathbf{u})
to denote the set of unique components of
\mathbf{u}
. For example, if
\mathbf{u}={(1,4,3,1,3)}^{T}
, then
A(\mathbf{u})=\{1,3,4\}
. Two graphs
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
with vertex sets
V(H_{1})
and
V(H_{2})
, and edge
sets
E(H_{1})
and
E(H_{2})
are said to be isomorphic to each other, if there exists a bijection
\phi:V(H_{1})\mapsto V(H_{2})
,
such that
\{u,v\}\in E(H_{1})
if and only if
\{\phi(u),\phi(v)\}\in E(H_{2})
. We use the notation
H_{1}\simeq H_{2}
to denote an isomorphism between
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
. If
V(H_{1})=V(H_{2})
and
H_{1}\simeq H_{2}
, then
\phi
is called an automorphism. If
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
are automorphic, then
E(H_{1})=E(H_{2})
. We now define the edge densities of
H
and its subgraphs. Fix a
t\in\{2,\ldots,R\}
and write let
B_{1},\ldots,B_{\binom{R}{t}}
denote the collection of all possible
t
-element subsets of
[R]
. Consider the induced subgraph of
H_{r}
of
H
, formed by the nodes in
B_{r}
,
i.e.
,
V(H_{r})=B_{r}
and
E(H_{r})=\left\{\{i,j\}\in E(H):~i,j\in B_{r}\right\}
. Define,

\displaystyle m(t:H)=\max\left\{|E(H_{r})|:r=1,\ldots,\binom{R}{t}\right\},\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for all $t=2,\ldots,R$.}

(3.2)

Thus,
m(t:H)
is the maximum number of edges contained in
any

t
-vertex induced subgraph of
H
, and
m(t:H)/t
denotes the highest
edge density
arising from any such
t
-vertex induced subgraph. It can be computed for any given choice of
t
and
H
and analytical expressions can be found for specific types of
H
. Note, the maximum in (
3.2
) can be attained at different choices of
H_{r}
. The
maximum edge density of
H
(see pp. 56 of
Janson et al. (2000)
) is defined as

\displaystyle m(H)=\max_{2\leq t\leq R}\frac{m(t:H)}{t}.

(3.3)

We will define
m(1:H)=0
, for any choice of
H
. A graph is said to be
strictly balanced
if
t\mapsto m(t:H)/t
, is
uniquely maximized
at
t=R
(see pp. 64 of
Janson et al. (2000)
). Most common target graphs are strictly balanced,
viz.
, complete graph,
star graph, line graph. The
vertex cover
(see pp. 36 of
Diestel (2018)
) of a graph
H
is a set
D\subseteq V(H)
such that

\{u,v\}\in E(H)\hskip 10.00002pt\Rightarrow\hskip 10.00002ptu\in D~\text{or}~v\in D.

A set
D\subseteq V(H)
is called a
minimum vertex cover
of
H
if,
D
is a vertex cover of
H
and for any other vertex cover
\tilde{D}
of
H
,
|D|\leq|\tilde{D}|
. A minimum vertex cover set need not be unique. The
size of the minimum vertex cover
of
H
is defined as

\tau(H)=|D|,\hskip 10.00002pt\text{where $D$ is a minimum vertex cover of $H$.}

(3.4)

One can show that
\tau(H)=1
if and only if
H
is a star graph (see Lemma
D.16
).

Let
S_{N}(H)
,
\widehat{S}_{N,1}(H)
and
\widehat{S}_{N,2}(H)
denote the subgraph counts for
H
in the population network
G_{N}
, within the sample based network formed by the induced approach, and within the sample based network formed by the ego-centric approach, respectively. They can be expressed as

\displaystyle S_{N}(H)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in{[N]}_{R}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E(H)}Y_{s_{i},s_{j}},\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\widehat{S}_{N,l}(H)=\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in{[N]}_{R}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E(H)}h_{l}\left(W_{N,s_{i}},W_{N,s_{j}}\right)\cdot Y_{s_{i},s_{j}},\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for $l=1,2$,}

(3.5)

where,
h_{l}
,
l=1,2
, are defined in (
2.8
). The standardized sample based subgraph counts
T_{N,l}(H)
and
T_{N,2}(H)
have been defined in (
1.3
). Let
{W}_{1},\ldots,{W}_{R}
, denote a collection of i.i.d. Bernoulli
(p)
random variables, where
p\in(0,1)
. For a given target graph
H
, with edge set
E(H)
, we define

\displaystyle f_{l}(p:H)=\mathbf{E}\left(\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E(H)}h_{l}\left({W}_{i},{W}_{j}\right)\right),\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for $l=1,2$.}

(3.6)

where
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
are defined in (
2.8
). Since
H
is connected (with
R=|V(H)|
), we can write

\displaystyle f_{1}(p:H)=p^{R}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptf_{2}(p:H)=\sum_{D\in\mathcal{D}}p^{|D|}\cdot{(1-p)}^{(R-|D|)},

(3.7)

where
\mathcal{D}
is the collection of all vertex covers of
H
. When
p_{N}\rightarrow 0
, then
f_{2}(p_{N}:H)\sim p^{\tau(H)}_{N}
.
The pivotal quantities
T_{N,l}(H)
,
l=1,2
, are defined in (
1.3
). The
d_{1}
distance between two real valued random variables
X
and
Y
is defined as (see pp. 158 of
Janson et al. (2000)
and
Barbour et al. (1989)
)

\displaystyle d_{1}(X,Y)=\sup\left\{|\mathbf{E}h(X)-\mathbf{E}h(Y)|:\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\left(|h(x)|+|h^{(1)}(x)|\right)\leq 1\right\}.

(3.8)

The
d_{1}
distance uses the class of all bounded real valued maps
h:\mathbb{R}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
, with a bounded derivative as the class of test functions. Such a map
h
is also uniformly continuous. From Theorem 25.8 of
Billingsley (1995)
it now follows that convergence in the
d_{1}
distance implies convergence in distribution. Next, we describe a crucial assumption which allows us to connect sets of vertices within
V(H)
, which provide a minimum vertex cover of
H
and simultaneously form an induced subgraph of
H
with the highest possible number of edges.

(A.5)

Let
\mathcal{D}_{\min}(H)
denote the collection of all minimum vertex covers of
H
. For any
t\in\{2,\ldots,R\}
, define the following sub-collection of
t
vertex induced subgraphs of
H
which have the highest possible number of edges,

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{t,H}=\left\{H_{r}:r=1,\ldots,\binom{R}{t},\hskip 10.00002pt|E(H_{r})|=m(t:H)\right\},

(3.9)

where
m(t:H)
is defined in (
3.2
). For every
t\in\{2,\ldots,R\}
we assume the following:

(i)

If
t\geq\tau(H)
, there exists a
H_{r_{0}}\in\mathcal{G}_{t,H}
and a
D_{0}\in\mathcal{D}_{\min}(H)
, such that
D_{0}\subseteq V(H_{r_{0}})
.

(ii)

If
t<\tau(H)
, there exists a
H_{r_{0}}\in\mathcal{G}_{t,H}
and a
D_{0}\in\mathcal{D}_{\min}(H)
, such that
V(H_{r_{0}})\subseteq D_{0}
.

The choice of
D_{0}
is allowed to depend on
t
.

In case
t\geq\tau(H)
, Assumption
(A.5)
implies that there exists at least one collection of
t
vertices, say
B_{r_{0}}\subset[R]
, such that the corresponding induced subgraph
H_{r_{0}}
has
m(t:H)
edges and
B_{r_{0}}
also contains a minimum vertex cover of
H
. For
t<\tau(H)
, there exists a minimum vertex cover of
H
and a vertex collection
B_{r_{0}}
, such that
B_{r_{0}}
is contained within the minimum vertex cover and the induced subgraph
H_{r_{0}}
has
m(t:H)
edges. One can easily check that two of the most common target subgraphs, the complete graph
\mathbb{K}_{R}
and the star graph
\mathbb{K}_{1,R-1}
satisfy Assumption
(A.5)
. For other choices of
H
a direct verification is needed.

Theorem 3.1

(A bound on the
d_{1}
distance)
.

Let
Z\sim N(0,1)
. Recall the definitions of
g_{l,\alpha,\beta,H}(\cdot)
and
t_{l}(\alpha,\beta;H)
, for
l=1,2
, from (
1.5
). Suppose that Assumptions
(A.1)
,
(A.2)
,
(A.3)
and
(A.4)
hold. Fix any
\alpha,\beta\geq 0
and consider any target subgraph
H
. For both
l=1
(induced case) and
l=2
(ego-centric case), there exists a constant
b_{l}\in(0,\infty)
and a positive integer
N_{0,l}
, which depend on the model parameters, sampling and model sparsity levels
\alpha
and
\beta
, and the edge structure of the target subgraph
H
, such that

\displaystyle d_{1}\left(T_{N,l}(H),Z\right)=\Delta_{N,l}(H;\alpha,\beta)\leq b_{l}\cdot\displaystyle N^{~\displaystyle\left\{g_{l,\alpha,\beta,H}\left(t_{l}(\alpha,\beta;H)\right)/2\right\}\displaystyle}\displaystyle,\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for $N\geq N_{0,l}$ and $l=1,2$.}

(3.10)

In addition to the above, if
\alpha>0
, then (
3.10
) holds in the ego-centric case (
l=2
), if the target subgraph
H
satisfies Assumption
(A.5)
.

The bound in (
3.10
) shows that if the exponent of
N
becomes negative, then the
d_{1}
distance
\Delta_{N,l}(H;\alpha,\beta)
,
l=1,2
, will converge to zero. The definition of
g_{l,\alpha,\beta,H}(\cdot)
in (
1.5
) shows that their values increase if the sparsity levels
\beta
and
\alpha
become higher. So, in order to ensure
g_{l,\alpha,\beta,H}(t_{l}(\alpha,\beta;H))<0
, for
l=1,2
, we need to place upper thresholds on the sparsity levels. Precisely the same is ensured if
(\alpha,\beta)\in C_{l}(H)
, for
l=1
and
l=2
(see (
1.6
) and (
1.7
)). For example, in the ego-centric case, from the definition of
g_{2,\alpha,\beta,H}(\cdot)
in (
1.5
) we can write

\displaystyle g_{2,\alpha,\beta,H}(t)

\displaystyle=t\cdot\left[-1+\frac{\alpha}{\frac{t}{\min\{\tau(H),t\}}}+\frac{\beta}{\frac{t}{m(t:H)}}\right]<0,\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for $t=2,\ldots,R$,}

(3.11)

and also at
t=1
, if
(\alpha,\beta)\in C_{2}(H)
. This is equivalent to
g_{2,\alpha,\beta,H}(t_{2}(\alpha,\beta;H))<0
. A similar argument works in the induced case. As a result, we obtain the following CLT for
T_{N,1}(H)
and
T_{N,2}(H)
.

Corollary 3.2

(CLT for sample based subgraph count estimates)
.

Assume that the conditions of Theorem
3.1
hold. If
(\alpha,\beta)\in C_{l}(H)
,
l=1,2
(cf. (
1.6
), (
1.7
)), then the following statements are true for
l=1,2
.

(a)

g_{l,\alpha,\beta,H}(t_{l}(\alpha,\beta;H))<0
, where
g_{l,\alpha,\beta,H}(\cdot)
,
l=1,2
, are defined in (
1.5
).

(b)

d_{1}(T_{N,l}(H),Z)=\Delta_{N,l}(H;\alpha,\beta)=O\left(N^{~\displaystyle\left\{g_{l,\alpha,\beta,H}\left(t_{l}(\alpha,\beta;H)\right)/2\right\}\displaystyle}\right)
, as
N\rightarrow\infty
.

(c)

T_{N,l}(H)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\rightarrow}}N(0,1)
, as
N\rightarrow\infty
, where
T_{N,l}(H)
is defined in (
1.3
).

Theorem
3.1
connects the decay rate of
d_{1}(T_{N,l}(H),Z)
with the sparsity level in the model, the sparsity level of the sampling scheme and the edge structure of the target subgraph
H
. It is true for
any
choice of sparsity levels
\beta
and
\alpha
. However, as shown in Corollary
3.2
, the bound is
effective
only when
(\alpha,\beta)\in C_{l}(H)
. One can show that the components of the sum involved in the pivotal quantity
T_{N,l}(H)
,
l=1,2
(cf. (
1.3
)) possess a dependency structure which satisfies the criteria provided in
Barbour et al. (1989)
. As stated above, the proof of Theorem
3.1
is based on the careful application of Theorem 6.32 of
Janson et al. (2000)
(also see Section 2 of
Barbour et al. (1989)
). We discuss some of the main challenges in the derivation of Theorem
3.1
. In order to use the above mentioned result from
Janson et al. (2000)
a key step is to obtain the
precise
growth rate of the variance term
\sigma^{2}_{N,l}(H)
(cf. (
1.3
)). For any
\mathbf{s}\in{[N]}_{R}
, write
W_{N,l}(\mathbf{s}:H)=\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E(H)}h_{l}\left(W_{N,s_{i}},W_{N,s_{j}}\right)
, for
l=1,2
, where
h_{l}(\cdot,\cdot)
,
l=1,2
, are defined in (
2.8
). For both
l=1,2
, we can write

\displaystyle\sigma^{2}_{N,l}(H)=\sum_{t=1}^{R}\sum_{(\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2})\in\mathcal{M}_{t}}\mathbf{cov}\left({W}_{N,l}(\mathbf{s}_{1}:H),{W}_{N,l}(\mathbf{s}_{2}:H)\right)\cdot\mathbf{E}\left[\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E(H(\mathbf{s}_{1})\cup H(\mathbf{s}_{2}))}Y_{i,j}\right],

(3.12)

where,
\mathcal{M}_{t}=\{(\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2}):\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2}\in{[N]}_{R},~|A(\mathbf{s}_{1})\cap A(\mathbf{s}_{2})|=t\}
is the collection of
(\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2})
pairs that have
t
common components,
H(\mathbf{s}_{1})
and
H(\mathbf{s}_{2})
denote graphs which are isomorphic to
H
and have vertex sets
V(H(\mathbf{s}_{1}))=A(\mathbf{s}_{1})
and
V(H(\mathbf{s}_{2}))=A(\mathbf{s}_{2})
and edge sets
E(H(\mathbf{s}_{1}))=\{\{s_{1,i},s_{1,j}\}:\{i,j\}\in E(H)\}
and
E(H(\mathbf{s}_{2}))=\{\{s_{2,i},s_{2,j}\}:\{i,j\}\in E(H)\}
, respectively. The union graph
H(\mathbf{s}_{1})\cup H(\mathbf{s}_{2})
has vertex set
A(\mathbf{s}_{1})\cup A(\mathbf{s}_{2})
and edge set
E(H(\mathbf{s}_{1}))\cup E(H(\mathbf{s}_{2}))
. The intersection graph
H(\mathbf{s}_{1})\cap H(\mathbf{s}_{2})
is defined in a similar way.

Using Assumptions
(A.3)
and
(A.4)
, it can be shown that in the induced case, the design based covariance term
\mathbf{cov}({W}_{N,1}(\mathbf{s}_{1}:H),W_{N,1}(\mathbf{s}_{2}:H))
in (
3.12
) is an increasing function of
|V(H(\mathbf{s}_{1})\cap H(\mathbf{s}_{2}))|
. Similarly, in the ego-centric case, this covariance decreases if
\tau(H(\mathbf{s}_{1})\cup H(\mathbf{s}_{2}))
increases. On the other hand, the order of magnitude of the model based expectation term involving
Y_{i,j}
’s in (
3.12
) is an increasing function of
|E(H(\mathbf{s}_{1})\cap H(\mathbf{s}_{2}))|
. Note, even if
|V(H(\mathbf{s}_{1})\cap H(\mathbf{s}_{2}))|=|A(\mathbf{s}_{1})\cap A(\mathbf{s}_{2})|
increases,
|E(H(\mathbf{s}_{1})\cap H(\mathbf{s}_{2}))|
may
not
increase and can even decrease. Due to the method in which
H(\mathbf{s}_{1})
and
H(\mathbf{s}_{2})
are created,
|E(H(\mathbf{s}_{1})\cap H(\mathbf{s}_{2}))|
will depend on the positioning of the common components within the vectors
\mathbf{s}_{1}
and
\mathbf{s}_{2}
. Similarly, no relation can be made between the values of
\tau(H(\mathbf{s}_{1})\cup H(\mathbf{s}_{2}))
and
|E(H(\mathbf{s}_{1})\cup H(\mathbf{s}_{2}))|=2T-|E(H(\mathbf{s}_{1})\cup H(\mathbf{s}_{2}))|
. Due to this reason, it is not possible to directly relate the dependencies between the sampling and model based components. These issues do not arise while studying the limit law of the population subgraph count
S_{N}(H)
(cf.
Nowicki and Wierman (1988)
) or when using a purely design based approach (cf.
Bhattacharya et al. (2022)
). To address this issue, one has to go through
all
possible structures of the union graph
H(\mathbf{s}_{1})\cup H(\mathbf{s}_{2})
. This can be achieved by further splitting
\mathcal{M}_{t}
in terms of the positions of the common components of
\mathbf{s}_{1}
and
\mathbf{s}_{2}
and how these common components are mapped among each other. To describe this splitting, first consider the following example.

Let
N=5
,
R=4
, and consider the vectors
\mathbf{s}_{1}={(2,5,3,4)}^{T}
and
\mathbf{s}_{2}={(1,4,5,2)}^{T}
. Thus,
(\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2})\in\mathcal{M}_{3}
and the common elements are
\{2,4,5\}
. Write
\mathbf{j}={(1,2,4)}^{T}
and
\mathbf{k}={(2,3,4)}^{T}
, to denote the positions at which these common elements are placed within
\mathbf{s}_{1}
and
\mathbf{s}_{2}
, respectively. Consider the permutation
\xi:\{1,2,3\}\mapsto\{1,2,3\}
, defined by
\xi(1)=3
,
\xi(2)=2
and
\xi(3)=1
. Then, we can write,
s_{1,j_{1}}=s_{2,k_{3}}=s_{2,k_{\xi(1)}}
,
s_{1,j_{2}}=s_{2,k_{2}}=s_{2,k_{\xi(2)}}
and
s_{1,j_{3}}=s_{2,k_{1}}=s_{2,k_{\xi(3)}}
. For any
t\in[R]
, let
\mathcal{S}_{t}
denote the collection of all permutation maps
\xi:[t]\mapsto[t]
. For any
\mathbf{j},\mathbf{k}\in{[R]}_{<,t}
(cf. (
3.1
)) and
\xi\in\mathcal{S}_{t}
, define

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{t}(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{k},\xi)=\left\{(\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2})\in\mathcal{M}_{t}:s_{1,j_{1}}=s_{2,k_{\xi(1)}},\ldots,s_{1,j_{t}}=s_{2,k_{\xi(t)}}\right\}.

(3.13)

It is easy to check that
\{\mathcal{M}_{t}(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{k},\xi):\mathbf{j},\mathbf{k}\in{[R]}_{<,t},\xi\in\mathcal{S}_{t}\}
will form a disjoint partition of
\mathcal{M}_{t}
, and for any
(\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2})\in\mathcal{M}_{t}(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{k},\xi)
, the edge structure of the union graph
H(\mathbf{s}_{1})\cup H(\mathbf{s}_{2})
will depend only on the underlying
(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{k},\xi)
, and not on the specific choice of
(\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2})
. This implies, for any
(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{k},\xi)
, we can study the edge structure of the union (or intersection) of
H(\mathbf{s}_{1})
and
H(\mathbf{s}_{2})
for any
(\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2})\in\mathcal{M}_{t}(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{k},\xi)
by considering the following representative pair of vectors
(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{1},\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{2})\in\mathcal{M}_{t}(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{k},\xi)
, where
A(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{1})=[R]
and
A(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{1})\cup A(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{2})=\{1,\ldots,2R-t\}
. Consider the union graph
H(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{1})\cup H(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{2})
. Then, for any
(\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2})\in\mathcal{M}_{t}(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{k},\xi)
, we can write

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left(W_{N,1}(\mathbf{s}_{1}:H)\cdot W_{N,1}(\mathbf{s}_{2}:H)\right)

\displaystyle=p^{|V(H(\mathbf{s}_{1})\cup H(\mathbf{s}_{2}))|}_{N}=p^{(2R-t)}_{N},\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left(W_{N,2}(\mathbf{s}_{1}:H)\cdot W_{N,2}(\mathbf{s}_{2}:H)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{D\in\mathcal{D}(H(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{1})\cup H(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{2}))}p^{|D|}_{N}{(1-p_{N})}^{(2R-t)-|D|},

where
\mathcal{D}(H(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{1})\cup H(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{2}))
is the collection of all vertex covers of the union graph
H(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{1})\cup H(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{2})
. Further, one can show that under the stated assumptions, for all choices of
(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{k},\xi)
and for large enough
N
,

\displaystyle\sum_{(\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2})\in\mathcal{M}_{t}(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{k},\xi)}\mathbf{E}\left[\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E(H(\mathbf{s}_{1})\cup H(\mathbf{s}_{2}))}Y_{i,j}\right]

\displaystyle=N^{2R-t}\cdot N^{-\beta\cdot(2T-|E(H(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{1})\cap H(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{2}))|)}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\times\Theta_{t}\left(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{k},\xi:\mathbf{C},\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{K},H\right)\cdot(1+o(1)),

where
\Theta_{t}\left(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{k},\xi:\mathbf{C},\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{K},H\right)
(cf. (
4.2
)) does not depend on
N
, and depends only on
(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{k},\xi)
,
\mathbf{C}
and
\{\lambda_{k}:k\in[K]\}
(cf. Assumptions
(A.2)
and
(A.3)
) and
H
. Continuing from (
3.12
), in the induced case (
l=1
) we can now write

\displaystyle\sigma^{2}_{N,1}(H)

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{R}\left(p^{2R-t}_{N}-p^{2R}_{N}\right)N^{2R-t}\sum_{(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{k},\xi)}N^{-\beta\cdot(2T-|E(H(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{1})\cap H(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{2}))|)}\cdot\Theta_{t}\left(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{k},\xi:\mathbf{C},\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{K}\right)(1+o(1)).

Now we can easily see that the rate of growth of
\sigma^{2}_{N,1}(H)
is determined by the maximum possible value of
|E(H(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{1})\cap H(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{2}))|
. In Lemma
D.5
we identify the precise set of
(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{k})
values where
|E(H(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{1})\cap H(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{2}))|
reaches its maximum possible value
m(t:H)
.

In the ego-centric case, for
\alpha=0
(with
p_{N}=c\in(0,1)
), the growth rate of
\sigma^{2}_{N,2}(H)
is influenced only by the highest value of
|E(H(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{1})\cap H(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{2}))|
and a similar argument can be applied. If
\alpha\in(0,1)
, the minimum vertex cover size of
H(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{1})\cup H(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{2})
begins to play a role. In this case, one can easily verify that for all
(\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2})\in\mathcal{M}_{t}(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{k},\xi)
, and large enough
N
,

\mathbf{E}\left(W_{N,2}(\mathbf{s}_{1}:H)\cdot W_{N,2}(\mathbf{s}_{2}:H)\right)=N^{-\alpha\cdot\tau(H(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{1})\cup H(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{2}))}\cdot O(1),

where
\tau(H(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{1})\cup H(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{2}))
denotes the minimum vertex cover size of
H(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{1})\cup H(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{2})
, and depends only on the choice of
(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{k},\xi)
. For large enough
N
and for
\alpha\in(0,1)
we can show that

\displaystyle\sigma^{2}_{N,2}(H)

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{R}N^{-2T\beta+2R-t}\sum_{(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{k},\xi)}N^{\displaystyle{}-\alpha\cdot\tau(H(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{1})\cup H(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{2}))+\beta\cdot|E(H(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{1})\cap H(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{2}))|\displaystyle}\cdot O(1)\cdot(1+o(1)).

Thus, the growth rate of
\sigma^{2}_{N,2}(H)
is determined by the choice of
(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{k},\xi)
, which maximizes the value of the linear combination

\displaystyle\psi_{t}(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{k},\xi:H)={}-\alpha\cdot\tau(H(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{1})\cup H(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{2}))+\beta\cdot|E(H(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{1})\cap H(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{2}))|.

(3.14)

Lemma
D.8
and Lemma
D.9
provide a lower bound for
\tau(H(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{1})\cup H(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{2}))
and also establish that this lower bound is attained at some triplet
(\mathbf{j}_{0},\mathbf{k}_{0},\xi_{0})
. However, there is
no guarantee
that
|E(H(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{1})\cap H(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{2}))|
will be maximized at
(\mathbf{j}_{0},\mathbf{k}_{0},\xi_{0})
. As a result, there is no way to analytically find the choice of
(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{k},\xi)
that maximizes
\psi_{t}(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{k},\xi:H)
. For specific choices of
H
, a brute-force search can be performed for every
t\in[R]
and
all
possible triplets
(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{k},\xi)\in{[R]}_{<,t}\times{[R]}_{<,t}\times\mathcal{S}_{t}
. The pair of values
\tau(H(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{1})\cup H(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{2}))
and
E|H(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{1})\cap H(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{2})|
has to be computed for each such triplet to find the maximizer of
\psi_{t}(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{k},\xi:H)
. Even this will become computationally infeasible for
R=10
(which requires a search over nearly 234 million triplets). Obviously, this is not feasible for any arbitrary choice of
H
. This is due to the fact that for any arbitrary graph
H
, the graph features
\tau(H)
and
|E(H)|
are not
coherent
with each other. This means, if an additional edge is included in
H
, it does not
necessarily
imply that
\tau(H)
will not decrease. Thus, it becomes impossible to find an analytical expression for the growth rate of
\sigma^{2}_{N,2}(H)
for any choice of
(\alpha,\beta)
and for any arbitrary choice of
H
. Consequently, Theorem 6.32 of
Janson et al. (2000)
cannot be used to establish a Gaussian limit law result for
T_{N,2}(H)
.

It is precisely at this stage where Assumption
(A.5)
helps us avoid this tedious enumeration and ensures the existence of a triplet
(\mathbf{j}_{0},\mathbf{k}_{0},\xi_{0})
, which will
simultaneously
minimize
\tau(H(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{1})\cup H(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{2}))
and maximize
E|H(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{1})\cap H(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{2})|
. This requires a special construction of the union graph
H(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{1})\cup H(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{2})
using an appropriate choice of
(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{k},\xi)
(see Lemma
D.9
and
D.8
). In addition to the above, in order to use Theorem 6.32 of
Janson et al. (2000)
we also establish an upper bound on the third order moments of
\left(\widehat{S}_{N,2}(H)-f_{2}(p_{N}:H)\cdot S_{N}(H)\right)
and then relate this to the previously found growth rate of
\sigma^{2}_{N,2}(H)
. For this task, we had to establish a lower bound on
\tau(\cup_{i=1}^{3}H(\mathbf{s}_{i}))
(see Lemma
D.8
) and then achieve control on
|E(\cup_{i=1}^{3}H(\mathbf{s}_{i}))|
by using a carefully constructed splitting of
V(\cup_{i=1}^{3}H(\mathbf{s}_{i}))
(see Lemma
D.6
and the proof of Theorem
3.1
). This had to be carried out for any
\mathbf{s}_{i}\in{[N]}_{R}
, for
i=1,2,3
, where, these vectors can have an arbitrary amount of overlap among themselves.

Now, we present some important aspects of the Gaussian approximation result presented in Theorem
3.1
and Corollary
3.2
.

Remark
1

(A special region of sparsity levels)
.

It is expected that if either the model or sampling sparsity levels increase, the quality of normal approximation for
T_{N,l}(H)
should degrade. However, our results show that for any choice of
H
, there exists a collection of model and sampling sparsity
\beta
and
\alpha
(that depend on
H
and the network formation mechanism), where the speed of normal approximation and the quality of statistical inference is
not
affected by the model sparsity level
\beta
, and is only affected by a change in the sampling sparsity level
\alpha
. In the case of the triangle, this corresponds to the regions
C_{1,1}(\mathbb{K}_{3})
and
C_{2,1}(\mathbb{K}_{3})
in Figure
1
(see (
1.9
) and (
1.10
)) in the induced and ego-centric cases respectively. Recall the definition of the sets
C_{l,j}(H)
(cf. (
1.8
)). It is easy to check, if
(\alpha,\beta)\in C_{l,1}(H)
, then
\Delta_{N,l}(H;\alpha,\beta)=O\left(N^{-(1-\alpha)/2}\right)
for
l=1,2
. For example, in the induced case (
l=1
), for any
H
,
C_{1,1}(H)=\{(\alpha,\beta):\alpha+m(t:H)\beta/(t-1)<1,\text{ for all $t=2,\ldots,R$}\}
. This implies, if the model sparsity level
\beta
is not too high,
i.e.
,
\beta<\min\left\{{(t-1)}/{m(t:H)}:2\leq t\leq R\right\}
, and we use dense sampling (
\alpha=0
), then the speed of normal approximation will be
O(N^{-1/2})
, and we will have the
same
inferential accuracy as in the case of a dense population network (
\beta=0
). The same conclusion works at any other
(\alpha,\beta)\in C_{1,1}(H)
with
\alpha>0
, except that the rate will be
O(N^{-(1-\alpha)/2})
. As the model sparsity level
\beta
increases beyond the thresholds provided in
C_{1,1}(H)
, the inferential precision starts to degrade very rapidly. For example, in the case of the triangle (cf. (
1.9
)) with
\alpha=0
, the decay rate stays at
O(N^{-1/2})
if
\beta<2/3
, but in between
\beta\in[2/3,1)
, the exponent of
N
in (
1.9
) increases from
-1/2
to
0
, as
\beta\uparrow 1
. The decay can be more rapid for other choices of
H
and a similar pattern emerges at any
\alpha>0
.

In the ego-centric case, if
\tau(H)=1
(
H=\mathbb{K}_{1,R-1}
, for some
R\geq 2
), then
C_{2,1}(\mathbb{K}_{1,R-1})={[0,1)}^{2}
and

C_{2}(\mathbb{K}_{1,R-1})=\{(\alpha,\beta):0\leq\alpha<1,~{\alpha}+(R-1)\beta<R\}
. Thus, for
any

R
vertex star graph, at any given sampling sparsity level
\alpha
, the inferential accuracy remains unaffected by the model sparsity level, as long as
\beta\in[0,1)
. The decay rate of
\Delta_{N,2}(\mathbb{K}_{1,R-1})
drops sharply when
\beta>1
, and the drop is faster for larger
R
. For any other
H
satisfying Assumption
(A.5)
, with
\tau(H)\geq 2
,

\displaystyle C_{2,1}(H)

\displaystyle=\left\{(\alpha,\beta):~\displaystyle\frac{\alpha}{\frac{t-1}{\min\{t,\tau(H)\}-1}}+\frac{\beta}{\frac{(t-1)}{m(t:H)}}<1\displaystyle,~\text{for $t=2,\ldots,R$,}\right\}.

Remark
2

(Comparing induced and ego-centric approaches)
.

The ego-centric network formation approach collects far more observations in comparison to the induced approach. However, at
\alpha=0
,
g_{1,0,\beta,H}(t)=g_{2,0,\beta,H}(t)
, for all
t\in[R]
(see (
1.5
)). This implies,
\Delta_{N,1}(H;0,\beta)
and
\Delta_{N,2}(H;0,\beta)
have the same decay rate at the same model sparsity level. As
p_{N}
remains bounded away from zero, enough number of nodes are sampled (as
N\rightarrow\infty
) in both the induced and ego-centric approaches, so that the ego-centric approach does not offer any advantages in terms of the speed of normal approximation. If
(\alpha,\beta)\in C_{1}(H)\cap C_{2}(H)
and
\alpha>0
, we note that
g_{1,\alpha,\beta,H}(t)\geq g_{2,\alpha,\beta,H}(t)
, for all
t\in[R]
(cf. (
1.5
)). This immediately implies that the maximum value achieved by
g_{1,\alpha,\beta,H}(\cdot)
will always be greater than or equal to the maximum value achieved by
g_{2,\alpha,\beta,H}(\cdot)
. On
C_{1}(H)\cap C_{2}(H)
, the maximum values of both maps are negative (see Corollary
3.2
(a)). This implies, if
(\alpha,\beta)\in C_{1}(H)\cap C_{2}(H)
, and if the sampling is sparse (
\alpha>0
), the ego-centric approach always provides a faster speed of normal approximation (cf. Corollary
3.2
(b)) and better inferential accuracy at any model sparsity level
\beta
. At
\alpha=1
, the induced approach collects a finite size of data, even as
N\rightarrow\infty
, implying that no meaningful inferential conclusions can be obtained. The ego-centric approach observes
O(N)
entries from the population adjacency matrix, allowing the possibility of developing inferential results.

In addition, the ego-centric approach also highlights finer differences among two different target graphs through the use of the minimum vertex cover size
\tau(H)
. For example, consider
H_{1}=\mathbb{K}_{1,3}
(star graph on
R=4
vertices) and
H_{2}=\mathbb{L}_{4}
(line on
R=4
vertices). Both graphs satisfy Assumption
(A.5)
and have the same edge densities, but
\tau(\mathbb{K}_{1,3})=1<\tau(\mathbb{L}_{4})=2
. The asymptotic properties of
T_{N,1}(H)
would be exactly similar for
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
. However, in the ego-centric case, due to the smaller value of
\tau(H_{1})
, we will have
C_{2}(\mathbb{L}_{4})\subset C_{2}(\mathbb{K}_{1,3})
, and the speed of normal approximation will be better for
T_{N,2}(H_{1})
. Intuitively, if
\tau(H)
is small, then it is easier to capture a copy of a graph
H
in
G_{N}
using the ego-centric approach.

Remark
3

(Continuity of the exponent of
N
in (
3.10
))
.

As
(\alpha,\beta)
vary over different sub-regions in
C_{1}(H)
and
C_{2}(H)
(cf. (
1.9
) and (
1.10
)), the exponent of
N
in (
3.10
),
g_{l,\alpha,\beta,H}(t_{l}(\alpha,\beta;H))
remains a continuous function of
(\alpha,\beta)
, even though
(\alpha,\beta)\mapsto t_{l}(\alpha,\beta;H)
can have jumps when
(\alpha,\beta)
vary over sub-regions of
C_{l}(H)
. This is due to the fact that choices of
t_{l}(\alpha,\beta;H)
are themselves determined by comparing
R
different linear maps of
(\alpha,\beta)
and as a result, the transitions in the values of
t_{l}(\alpha,\beta;H)
arise when
g_{l,\alpha,\beta,H}(t)\geq g_{l,\alpha,\beta,H}(t^{\prime})
, for some
t\neq t^{\prime}
. This ensures the continuity of
(\alpha,\beta)\mapsto g_{l,\alpha,\beta,H}(t_{l}(\alpha,\beta;H))
, for
l=1,2
.

Remark
4

(Comparison with the population subgraph count
S_{N}(H)
)
.

Using similar proof techniques, we can show that if
\beta\in[0,1/m(H))
, then for any choice of
H
,

\displaystyle d_{1}\left(\frac{S_{N}(H)-\mathbf{E}(S_{N}(H))}{\sqrt{\mathbf{Var}(S_{N}(H))}},Z\right)

\displaystyle=O\left(N^{\displaystyle g_{1,0,\beta,H}(t_{P}(\beta;H))/2\displaystyle}\right),\hskip 10.00002pt\text{where, $Z\sim N(0,1)$, and}

\displaystyle t_{P}(\beta;H)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{argmax}\left\{g_{1,0,\beta,H}(t):t=2,\ldots,R\right\}.

The same upper threshold on the model sparsity level was obtained by
Janson et al. (2000)
for sparse Erdős-Rényi random graphs. Unlike
t_{l}(\alpha,\beta;H)
(cf. (
1.5
)), the maximization is carried out over
t\in\{2,\ldots,R\}
, because in the model based setup, edge dependency can not arise if
|A(\mathbf{s}_{1})\cap A(\mathbf{s}_{2})|\leq 1
. This also impacts the speed of convergence to normality. For example, at
\beta=0
,
S_{N}(H)
converges in law to
N(0,1)
at the speed
O(N^{-1})
, for any
H
. In comparison, for the sampling based case, we have
\Delta_{N,l}(H;0,0)=O\left(N^{-1/2}\right)
. The speed of convergence to normality slows down by an order of magnitude in the case of the sample based subgraph counts, only because of the extra dependency arising from the effect of the sampling design.

Remark
5
.

Bhattacharya et al. (2022)
made a seminal contribution in the area of network sampling based inference. They used the so called
fourth moment convergence criterion
, to obtain a CLT for the induced sample based subgraph count
\widehat{S}_{N,1}(H)
in a purely design based framework, where the population network
G_{N}
is
non-stochastic
. They did not consider the ego-centric approach. Our results are not directly comparable to those obtained in
Bhattacharya et al. (2022)
, as we use a different inferential framework. However, some basic differences can be pointed out. Unlike the joint model and the design based variance expression in (
3.12
),
Bhattacharya et al. (2022)
used the design based variance for standardization. The proof techniques used in this article are not related to the approach used by
Bhattacharya et al. (2022)
. In fact, we do not require the use of fourth order moments of the pivotal quantity
T_{N,l}(H)
(cf. (
1.3
)), neither we need to verify its convergence to the fourth moment of a standard normal random variable. We utilize a Stein’s method based bound (cf. Theorem 6.32 of
Janson et al. (2000)
), which uses second and third order moments of the pivotal quantity
T_{N,l}(H)
. Interestingly, their proposed fourth moment convergence criterion is usable only when the node selection probability is less than or equal to
1/20
.

For the ease of practitioners, we now state the bounds on
\Delta_{N,l}(H;\alpha,\beta)
,
l=1,2
, for some commonly used target subgraphs
H
. The following graphs are considered: complete graph
\mathbb{K}_{R}
, star graph
\mathbb{K}_{1,R-1}
and line graph
\mathbb{L}_{R}
on
R
vertices. All the above mentioned graphs are strictly balanced. Assumption
(A.5)
is satisfied by any complete graph and star graph, but this assumption is not satisfied by a line graph if
R>4
. The edge (
H=\mathbb{K}_{2}
) is treated as a star graph on two vertices.

Corollary 3.3

(Decay rates for
\Delta_{N,l}(H;\alpha,\beta)
for specific graphs)
.

Suppose the conditions of Theorem
3.1
hold and recall the definition of
C_{l,j}(H)
(cf. (
1.8
)).

(a)

Consider the star graph
H=\mathbb{K}_{1,R-1}
on
R\geq 2
vertices, with
T=(R-1)
,
m(t:\mathbb{K}_{1,R-1})=(t-1)
, for
t\in\{2,\ldots,R\}
, and
\tau(\mathbb{K}_{1,R-1})=1
. In this case,
C_{1}(\mathbb{K}_{1,R-1})=\{(\alpha,\beta):\alpha+(R-1)\beta/R<1,~\alpha\in[0,1)\}
and
C_{2}(\mathbb{K}_{1,R-1})=\{(\alpha,\beta):\alpha/R+(R-1)\beta/R<1,~\alpha\in[0,1)\}
.
For any
(\alpha,\beta)\in C_{1}(\mathbb{K}_{1,R-1})
,

\displaystyle\Delta_{N,1}(\mathbb{K}_{1,R-1};\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}O\left(N^{-\frac{1}{2}(1-\alpha)}\right)&\hskip 9.24994pt\text{if $(\alpha,\beta)\in C_{1,1}(\mathbb{K}_{1,R-1})=\{(\alpha,\beta):0\leq\beta<1-\alpha\}$,}\\
O\left(N^{-\frac{(R-1)}{2}\left[\frac{R}{R-1}(1-\alpha)-\beta\right]}\right)&\hskip 9.24994pt\text{if $(\alpha,\beta)\in C_{1,R}(\mathbb{K}_{1,R-1})=\{(\alpha,\beta):1-\alpha\leq\beta<\frac{R(1-\alpha)}{R-1},\alpha\in[0,1)\}$.}\end{cases}

For any
(\alpha,\beta)\in C_{2}(\mathbb{K}_{1,R-1})
,
