\displaystyle\Delta_{N,2}(\mathbb{K}_{1,R-1};\alpha,\beta)=\begin{cases}O\left(N^{-(1-\alpha)/2}\right)&\ \text{if $(\alpha,\beta)\in C_{2,1}(\mathbb{K}_{1,R-1})={[0,1)}^{2}$,}\\
O\left(N^{-\frac{(R-1)}{2}\left[\frac{(R-\alpha)}{R-1}-\beta\right]}\right)&\ \text{if $(\alpha,\beta)\in C_{2,R}(\mathbb{K}_{1,R-1})=\left\{(\alpha,\beta):1\leq\beta<\frac{(R-\alpha)}{R-1},\alpha\in[0,1)\right\}$.}\end{cases}

The expressions for
\sigma^{2}_{N,1}(\mathbb{K}_{1,R-1})
and
\sigma^{2}_{N,2}(\mathbb{K}_{1,R-1})
are provided in (
E.163
) and (
E.164
) respectively (see Appendix
Appendix E: Variance expressions for some common target subgraphs
).

(b)

Consider the complete graph
H=\mathbb{K}_{R}
on
R\geq 3
vertices, with
T=R(R-1)/2
,
m(t:H)=t(t-1)/2
, for
t\in\{2,\ldots,R\}
and
\tau(\mathbb{K}_{R})=(R-1)
. In this case,
C_{1}(\mathbb{K}_{R})=\{(\alpha,\beta):\alpha+\frac{(R-1)\beta}{2}<1,\alpha\in[0,1)\}
and

\displaystyle C_{2}(\mathbb{K}_{R})

\displaystyle=\left\{(\alpha,\beta):\alpha+\frac{(R-2)\beta}{2}<1,\frac{(R-1)\alpha}{R}+\frac{(R-1)\beta}{2}<1,~\alpha\in[0,1)\right\}.

For any
(\alpha,\beta)\in C_{1}(\mathbb{K}_{R})
,

\displaystyle\Delta_{N,1}(\mathbb{K}_{R};\alpha,\beta)=\begin{cases}O\left(N^{-\frac{1}{2}(1-\alpha)}\right)&\ \text{if $(\alpha,\beta)\in C_{1,1}(\mathbb{K}_{R})=\left\{(\alpha,\beta):0\leq\beta<\frac{2(1-\alpha)}{R}\right\}$,}\\
O\left(N^{-\frac{R(R-1)}{4}\left(\frac{2}{R-1}(1-\alpha)-\beta\right)}\right)&\ \text{if $(\alpha,\beta)\in C_{1,R}(\mathbb{K}_{R})=\left\{(\alpha,\beta):\frac{2(1-\alpha)}{R}\leq\beta<\frac{2(1-\alpha)}{R-1}\right\}$.}\end{cases}

Define the following sub-regions within
C_{2}(\mathbb{K}_{R})
,

\displaystyle C_{2,1}(\mathbb{K}_{R})

\displaystyle=\left\{(\alpha,\beta):\alpha+\frac{\beta}{2/(R-1)}<1,~\frac{\alpha}{(R-1)/(R-2)}+\frac{\beta}{2/R}<1,\alpha\in[0,1)\right\},

\displaystyle C_{2,R-1}(\mathbb{K}_{R})

\displaystyle=\left\{(\alpha,\beta):\alpha+\frac{\beta}{2/(R-1)}>1,~\beta<\frac{1}{R-1},~\alpha+\frac{\beta}{2/(R-2)}<1,\alpha\in[0,1)\right\},\hskip 8.50012pt\text{and}

\displaystyle C_{2,R}(\mathbb{K}_{R})

\displaystyle=\left\{(\alpha,\beta):\frac{\alpha}{(R-1)/(R-2)}+\frac{\beta}{2/R}>1,~\beta>\frac{1}{R-1},~\frac{(R-1)\alpha}{R}+\frac{(R-1)\beta}{2}<1,\alpha\in[0,1)\right\}.

For any
(\alpha,\beta)\in C_{2}(\mathbb{K}_{R})
,

\displaystyle\Delta_{N,2}(\mathbb{K}_{R};\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}O\left(N^{-\frac{1}{2}(1-\alpha)}\right)&\text{if $(\alpha,\beta)\in C_{2,1}(\mathbb{K}_{R})$,}\\
O\left(N^{\frac{1}{2}\left(-(R-1)(1-\alpha)+\frac{\beta}{2}(R-1)(R-2)\right)}\right)&\text{if $(\alpha,\beta)\in C_{2,R-1}(\mathbb{K}_{R})$,}\\
O\left(N^{\frac{1}{2}\left(-R+(R-1)\alpha+\frac{\beta}{2}R(R-1)\right)}\right)&\text{if $(\alpha,\beta)\in C_{2,R}(\mathbb{K}_{R})$.}\end{cases}

The expression for
\sigma^{2}_{N,1}(\mathbb{K}_{R})
and
\sigma^{2}_{N,2}(\mathbb{K}_{R})
are provided in (
E.165
) and (
E.166
) respectively (see Appendix
Appendix E: Variance expressions for some common target subgraphs
).

(c)

Consider the line graph
H=\mathbb{L}_{R}
on
R
vertices, with
R\geq 4
. Here,
T=(R-1)
,
m(t:\mathbb{L}_{R})=(t-1)
, for
t\in\{2,\ldots,R\}
.
In this case,
C_{1}(\mathbb{L}_{R})=C_{1}(\mathbb{K}_{1,R-1})
(see part (a) above).
The expression for
\sigma^{2}_{N,1}(\mathbb{L}_{R})
is provided in (
E.167
) (see Appendix
Appendix E: Variance expressions for some common target subgraphs
).

3.1
Poisson-based limit laws for estimated subgraph counts

In this section, we investigate the limit laws of
T_{N,1}(H)
and
T_{N,2}(H)
, when the model and sampling sparsity levels reach the boundaries of
C_{1}(H)
and
C_{2}(H)
respectively. We only focus on target graphs
H
which are strictly balanced,
i.e.
,
m(t:H)
(cf. (
3.2
)) is uniquely maximized at
t=R
, and
m(H)=T/R
(cf. (
3.3
)). In addition, for the ego-centric case, for
\alpha>0
, we consider
H
satisfying Assumption
(A.5)
and for
\alpha=0
, all choices of
H
are allowed. If
H
is not strictly balanced, it is not clear if any proper limit law for
T_{N,l}(H)
can be obtained at the boundary points of
C_{l}(H)
for
l=1,2
. Most common target graphs of interest are strictly balanced.
Janson et al. (2000)
obtained Poisson based limit laws for
S_{N}(H)
in the case of sparse Erdős-Rényi random graphs at the extreme model sparsity level
\beta=R/T
. They obtained these results only in the case of strictly balanced
H
. Let
C^{b}_{l}(H)
denote the boundary of
C_{l}(H)
,
l=1,2
(cf. (
1.6
), (
1.7
)). As
H
is strictly balanced, splitting
C^{b}_{1}(H)
in terms of the level of sampling sparsity (dense, intermediate and extreme), we can write

\displaystyle C^{b}_{1}(H)=\{(0,R/T)\}\cup\left\{\left(\alpha,{R(1-\alpha)}/{T}\right):\alpha\in(0,1)\right\}\cup\{(1,0)\}.

Figures
1
(a) and
2
(a) show
C^{b}_{1}(\mathbb{K}_{3})
and
C^{b}_{1}(\mathbb{K}_{1,3})
respectively. In the ego-centric case, at
\tau(H)=1
, we can similarly split,

\displaystyle C^{b}_{2}(\mathbb{K}_{1,R-1})=\left\{\left(0,{R}/{(R-1)}\right)\right\}\cup\left\{\left(\alpha,{(R-\alpha)}/{(R-1)}\right):\alpha\in(0,1)\right\}\cup\{(1,\beta):0\leq\beta\leq 1\}.

Figure
2
(b) displays
C^{b}_{2}(\mathbb{K}_{1,3})
. When
\tau(H)>1
,
C^{b}_{2}(H)
is defined by the points of intersection of the lines

\displaystyle\min\{t,\tau(H)\}\cdot{\alpha}+m(t:H)\cdot{\beta}=t,\hskip 10.00002ptt=2,\ldots,R.

(3.15)

The boundary
C^{b}_{2}(H)
starts at
(\alpha,\beta)=(0,R/T)
, follows the equation of the line at
t=R
within the collection of lines (
3.15
), till it intersects another line from (
3.15
) at an intermediate point
(\alpha^{\star}_{H},\beta^{\star}_{H})
. For
j=1,\ldots,(R-1)
, let
(\alpha_{R,j},\beta_{R,j})
denote the point of intersection of the line at
t=R
with the line at
t=j
from the collection in (
3.15
). Then

\displaystyle\alpha^{\star}_{H}=\min\{\alpha_{R,j}:j=1,\ldots,(R-1)\}=\min_{j\in\{1,\ldots,R-1\}}\frac{j-\frac{R}{T}\cdot m(j:H)}{\min\{\tau(H),j\}-\frac{\tau(H)}{T}\cdot m(j:H)}.

(3.16)

It can be checked that
\alpha^{\star}_{H}\in(0,1)
, for any choice of
H
(with
\tau(H)>1
) and it can be easily computed for any choice of
H
. The boundary
C^{b}_{2}(H)
continues as per the above procedure and the last line segment always ends at
(\alpha,\beta)=(1,0)
. For example, in the case of
H=\mathbb{K}_{3}
, Figure
1
(b) shows that the boundary of
C_{2}(\mathbb{K}_{3})
consists of only two line segments with
\alpha^{\star}_{\mathbb{K}_{3}}=3/4
. We now summarize our findings on Poisson-based limit laws based on the underlying sampling sparsity level
\alpha
.

(i)

Dense case (
\alpha=0
):
Theorem
3.4
(a) establishes a Poisson based limit law at the boundary point
(\alpha,\beta)=(0,R/T)
, for
any

H
, in both induced and ego-centric cases. These two limit laws are similar and differ in their underlying parameters. They are expressible as a weighted sum of two independent Poisson random variables. We use a multivariate Poisson approximation result, developed by
Nualart and Zhang (2025)
to obtain this result. It involves analyzing the joint limiting behavior of the pair of counts
(\widehat{S}_{N,l}(H),S_{N}(H))
, where both components are
dependent
and both converge marginally to different Poisson distributions.
In Figures
1
and
2
, this specific limit law arises at the solid black dotted points
(0,1)
and
(0,4/3)
respectively.

(ii)

Intermediate case (
\alpha\in(0,1)
):
In this scenario, a second type of Poisson-based limit law is obtained for
T_{N,1}(H)
, for any
H
that satisfies Assumption
(A.5)
, on the boundary
C^{b}_{1}(H)
at
all
choices of
\alpha\in(0,1)
(see Theorem
3.4
(b)). This limit law is expressible in terms of a scaled and centered Poisson random variable. In Figures
1
(a) and
2
(a), this corresponds to points on the solid red line, at the boundary of the sub-regions
C_{1,3}(\mathbb{K}_{3})
and
C_{1,4}(\mathbb{K}_{1,3})
respectively.

In the ego-centric case, we are able to obtain the same limit law (with different parameters) for
T_{N,2}(H)
(cf. Theorem
3.4
(b)) on the boundary
C^{b}_{2}(H)
, when
\alpha\in(0,\widetilde{\alpha}_{H})
, where

\displaystyle\widetilde{\alpha}_{H}=\begin{cases}1&\text{if $\tau(H)=1$,}\\
\alpha^{\star}_{H}&\text{if $\tau(H)>1$,}\end{cases}

(3.17)

where
\alpha^{\star}_{H}
is defined in (
3.16
). This implies, in the case of a star graph
H=\mathbb{K}_{1,R-1}
, the limit law for
T_{N,2}(\mathbb{K}_{1,R-1})
on the boundary
C^{b}_{2}(\mathbb{K}_{1,R-1})
is obtained for the
entire
range of sampling sparsity levels
\alpha\in(0,1)
. In case of other graphs
H
with
\tau(H)>1
, our results are available for
\alpha\in(0,\alpha^{\star}_{H})
, which provides a
restricted
range of sampling sparsity levels. See Theorem
3.4
(b) for more details. The existence and nature of limiting distributions of
T_{N,2}(H)
on the boundary
C^{b}_{2}(H)
, for
\tau(H)>1
and
\alpha\geq\alpha^{\star}_{H}
, is not yet known and needs to be investigated. Theorem
3.4
(b) is obtained by using a well know total-variation distance bound for Poisson approximation (see Theorem 6.23 of
Janson et al. (2000)
). The solid red line on top of the sub-region
C_{2,2}(\mathbb{K}_{1,3})
in Figure
2
(b), displays the boundary region
C^{b}_{2}(\mathbb{K}_{1,3})
for
\alpha\in(0,1)
. The solid red line on top of the sub-region
C_{2,3}(\mathbb{K}_{3})
in Figure
1
(b) displays the boundary region
C^{b}_{2}(\mathbb{K}_{3})
for
\alpha\in(0,\alpha^{\star}_{\mathbb{K}_{3}})
, where
(\alpha^{\star}_{\mathbb{K}_{3}},\beta^{\star}_{\mathbb{K}_{3}})=(3/4,1/2)
.

(iii)

Extreme case (
\alpha=1
):
At this sampling sparsity level, in the induced case, only a finite amount of data is observed, even when the population size
N\rightarrow\infty
. This is true at all levels of model sparsity. This implies, there is no possibility of meaningful statistical inference using extremely sparse sampling and induced network formation, for any choice of
H
.

The ego-centric approach provides an opportunity to obtain inferential conclusions even when
\alpha=1
. However, our existing results are limited in scope. We have been able to establish a third type of Poisson based limit law for
T_{N,2}(\mathbb{K}_{1,R-1})
on
C^{b}_{2}(\mathbb{K}_{1,R-1})
, when the model sparsity level
\beta\in[0,1)
. This limit law is expressible as linear combination of
K
independent Poisson random variables (see Theorem
3.4
(c)) and is obtained by a characteristic function argument. We are unable to derive the limit law of
T_{N,2}(\mathbb{K}_{1,R-1})
at the corner point
(\alpha,\beta)=(1,1)
(see Figure
2
(b)).

For
\tau(H)>1
, the existence and nature of limiting distributions of
T_{N,2}(H)
on the boundary
C^{b}_{2}(H)
, at
\alpha=1
, is not yet known and needs to be investigated. Refer to Figure
1
(b), which displays a region
U
, where the nature of limit laws of
T_{N,2}(H)
remains unknown.

Now we state the main result on Poisson based limit laws for
T_{N,l}(H)
,
l=1,2
, at boundary points of
C_{1}(H)
and
C_{2}(H)
.

Theorem 3.4

(Poisson based limit laws for
T_{N,l}(H)
)
.

Suppose that Assumptions
(A.1)
,
(A.2)
,
(A.3)
and
(A.4)
hold. Assume
H
is a strictly balanced target subgraph with
m(H)=T/R
(cf. (
3.2
)).

(a)

Dense sampling:
At
\alpha=0
,
p_{N}=c\in(0,1)
for all
N\geq 1
(cf. (
2.6
)), and the only boundary point in
C_{l}(H)
,
l=1,2
, is
(\alpha,\beta)=(0,R/T)
, for both
l=1,2
. Recall the definitions of
f_{l}(\cdot:H)
,
l=1,2
,
\mathbf{C}
and
\{\lambda_{k}:k=1,\ldots,K\}
from (
3.7
), (
2.5
) and Assumption
(A.2)
respectively. Define the constant

\displaystyle\kappa(\mathbf{C},\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{K}:H)

\displaystyle=\sum_{u_{1},\ldots,u_{R}\in[K]}\lambda_{u_{1}}\cdot\lambda_{u_{2}}\cdots\lambda_{u_{R}}\cdot\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E(H)}c_{u_{i},u_{j}}.

(3.18)

For
l=1
and
l=2
, we define two independent Poisson random variables
U_{1,l,H}
and
U_{2,l,H}
with means
f_{l}(c:H)\cdot\kappa(\mathbf{C},\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{K}:H)
and
\left\{1-f_{l}(c:H)\right\}\cdot\kappa(\mathbf{C},\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{K}:H)
, respectively. Then,

\displaystyle T_{N,l}(H)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\rightarrow}}\frac{\left(1-f_{l}(c:H)\right)\cdot U_{1,l,H}-f_{l}(c:H)\cdot U_{2,l,H}}{[f_{l}(c:H)\cdot\{1-f_{l}(c:H)\}\cdot\kappa(\mathbf{C},\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{K}:H)]^{1/2}},\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for $l=1,2$.}

(3.19)

(b)

Intermediately sparse sampling:
Consider the following regions of boundary values within
C_{1}(H)
and
C_{2}(H)
, corresponding to intermediate sampling sparsity levels:

\displaystyle F_{1}(H)

\displaystyle=\left\{(\alpha,\beta):0<\alpha<1,~\beta=\frac{R\cdot(1-\alpha)}{T}\right\},\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}

(3.20)

\displaystyle F_{2}(H)

\displaystyle=\left\{(\alpha,\beta):0<\alpha<\widetilde{\alpha}_{H},~\beta=\left(1-\frac{\tau(H)\cdot\alpha}{R}\right)\cdot\frac{R}{T}\right\},

where,
\widetilde{\alpha}_{H}
is defined in (
3.17
). In the ego-centric case (for
l=2
), assume that
H
satisfies Assumption
(A.5)
. Recall that
p_{N}=c/N^{\alpha}
(cf. (
2.6
)). Define the maps,
\phi_{1}(c:H)=c^{R}
and
\phi_{2}(c:H)=c^{\tau(H)}
, for any
c>0
. Define two Poisson random variables
V_{1,H}
and
V_{2,H}
, with means
\phi_{1}(c:H)\cdot\kappa(\mathbf{C},\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{K}:H)
and
\phi_{2}(c:H)\cdot\kappa(\mathbf{C},\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{K}:H)
, respectively, where
\kappa(\mathbf{C},\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{K}:H)
is defined in (
3.18
). Then,

T_{N,l}(H)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\rightarrow}}\frac{V_{l,H}-\phi_{l}(c:H)\cdot\kappa(\mathbf{C},\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{K}:H)}{\left[\phi_{l}(c:H)\cdot\kappa(\mathbf{C},\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{K}:H)\right]^{1/2}},\hskip 10.00002pt\text{if $(\alpha,\beta)\in F_{l}(H)$, for both $l=1,2$.}

(3.21)

(c)

Extremely sparse sampling with
\tau(H)=1
:
Consider
H=\mathbb{K}_{1,R-1}
, with
\tau(H)=1
and assume that
\alpha=1
and
\beta\in[0,1)
. Let
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{K}
denote a collection of independent Poisson random variables with means
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{K}
, respectively (see Assumption
(A.2)
). Then

\displaystyle T_{N,2}(H)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\rightarrow}}\frac{1}{[\nu(\mathbf{C},\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{K}:H)]^{1/2}}\sum_{u_{1}=1}^{K}(Z_{u_{1}}-c\cdot\lambda_{u_{1}})\cdot\left(\sum_{u_{2},\ldots,u_{R}\in[K]}c_{u_{1},u_{2}}\cdots c_{u_{1},u_{R}}\times\lambda_{u_{2}}\cdots\lambda_{u_{R}}\right),

(3.22)

\displaystyle\text{where,}\hskip 10.00002pt\nu\left(\mathbf{C},\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{K}:H\right)=c\cdot\sum_{u_{1}=1}^{K}\lambda_{u_{1}}\left(\sum_{u_{2},\ldots,u_{R}\in[K]}c_{u_{1},u_{2}}\cdots c_{u_{1},u_{R}}\times\lambda_{u_{2}}\cdots\lambda_{u_{R}}\right)^{2}.

Remark
6

(Limiting behavior of
T_{N,l}(H)
beyond the boundaries of
C_{l}(H)
)
.

An important question is, what happens to the limit law of
T_{N,l}(H)
,
l=1,2
, when the sparsity levels
(\alpha,\beta)
go beyond the boundaries of
C_{l}(H)
,
l=1,2
? We are able to provide partial answers to this question, and we focus only on strictly balanced
H
. Firstly, in the induced case, it can be shown that,
\widehat{S}_{N,1}(H)-f_{1}(p_{N}:H)\cdot S_{N}(H)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\rightarrow}}0
, if
\alpha+m(H)\cdot\beta>1
and
\alpha\in[0,1]
. For example, in Figures
1
(a) and
2
(a), this is the region above the solid red line. Similarly, in the ego-centric case, for
\tau(H)=1
(star graph), we can show
\widehat{S}_{N,2}(\mathbb{K}_{1,R-1})-f_{2}(p_{N}:\mathbb{K}_{1,R-1})\cdot S_{N}(\mathbb{K}_{1,R-1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\rightarrow}}0
, if
{\alpha}/{R}+(R-1)\cdot{\beta}/R>1
and
\alpha\in[0,1]
. In Figure
2
(b), this is the region above the solid red line, including the region above the point
(\alpha,\beta)=(1,1)
. The situation becomes complicated if
\tau(H)>1
. In this case if we consider
\alpha\in[0,\alpha^{\star}_{H})
(cf. (
3.16
)), then the boundary of
C_{2}(H)
, on this range of
\alpha
values is the line,
\tau(H)\cdot\alpha+T\cdot\beta=R
. It can be shown

\widehat{S}_{N,2}(H)-f_{2}(p_{N}:H)\cdot S_{N}(H)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\rightarrow}}0,\hskip 10.00002pt\text{if $\tau(H)\cdot\alpha+T\cdot\beta>R$ and $\alpha\in[0,\alpha^{\star}_{H})$.}

If
\alpha\in[\alpha^{\star}_{H},1]
and
(\alpha,\beta)
exceed (or are on) the boundary of
C_{2}(H)
, then the limit law of
T_{N,2}(H)
is currently unknown (provided it exists). For example, this scenario arises on the region
U
, shown in Figure
1
(b) for
H=\mathbb{K}_{3}
. If
(\alpha,\beta)
keeps increasing further, so that the point
(\alpha,\beta)
is ‘higher’ than all the
(R-1)
lines provided in (
3.15
), then
\widehat{S}_{N,2}(H)-f_{2}(p_{N}:H)\cdot S_{N}(H)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\rightarrow}}0
, irrespective of the value of
\alpha
. In Figure
1
(b), this happens when
(\alpha,\beta)
is above the boundary of
C_{2,3}(\mathbb{K}_{3})
and also above the region
U
.

Remark
7

(Necessary conditions for a Gaussian limit law)
.

In the induced case and in the ego-centric case for the star graph, the thresholds on the sparsity levels, provided in
C_{1}(H)
and
C_{2}(\mathbb{K}_{1,R-1})
are not only sufficient, but they are also
necessary
to achieve a Gaussian limit law. This can be clearly seen from the shape of the boundary regions, where Poisson based limit laws are found. In the ego-centric case, if
\tau(H)>1
, the thresholds in
C_{2}(H)
are also necessary (if we leave out the case of
\alpha\geq\alpha^{\star}_{H}
, see (
3.16
)). Even then, in the case of the triangle (
H=\mathbb{K}_{3}
), we conjecture that
T_{N,2}(\mathbb{K}_{3})

cannot
have a Gaussian limit law on the region
U
(see Figure
1
(b)).
Janson et al. (2000)
exhibit similar sharp transitions from Gaussian to Poisson based limit laws for the population subgraph count
S_{N}(H)
in the case of Erdős-Rényi random graphs, as the model sparsity level
\beta
increases. However, it is remarkable to find that a similar sharp transition is observed even in the case of estimated subgraph counts, for both induced and ego-centric approaches, as either one or both of the model and sampling sparsity levels increases.

Remark
8

(Extensions to other models)
.

Based on the techniques used for proving our main results, we believe that the above described Gaussian and Poisson based limit law results for the estimated subgraph counts can be extended to other population network models, including popular variants of the SBM, such as the degree corrected SBM (
Karrer and Newman (2011)
), or for random dot product graph models (
Athreya et al. (2018)
), at the cost of more complicated technical assumptions. We plan to investigate these issues in a future article.

(a) Induced case:
C_{1}(\mathbb{K}_{1,3})
and its sub-regions.

(b) Ego-centric case:
C_{2}(\mathbb{K}_{1,3})
and its sub-regions.

Figure 2
:
Sparsity levels and limit laws for estimated star graph counts:

T_{N,1}(\mathbb{K}_{1,3})
and
T_{N,2}(\mathbb{K}_{1,3})
have a Gaussian limit on
C_{1}(\mathbb{K}_{1,3})
and
C_{2}(\mathbb{K}_{1,3})
(yellow shaded regions), with different decay rates for
\Delta_{N,l}(\mathbb{K}_{1,3};\alpha,\beta)
,
l=1,2
, within each sub-region. Similar Poisson based limit laws arise at
(\alpha,\beta)=(0,R/T)=(0,4/3)
(marked by filled black dot on both figures) for both
T_{N,l}(\mathbb{K}_{1,3})
,
l=1,2
, see Theorem
3.4
(a). A different Poisson based limit law arises on the upper boundaries of
C_{1,4}(\mathbb{K}_{1,3})
and
C_{2,2}(\mathbb{K}_{1,3})
(marked by solid red line), see Theorem
3.4
(b). Another Poisson based limit law arises for
T_{N,2}(\mathbb{K}_{1,3})
on the line
\alpha=1
, for
\beta\in[0,1)
, see solid blue line in figure (b) and Theorem
3.4
(c). Limit law of
T_{N,2}(\mathbb{K}_{1,3})
is unknown when
(\alpha,\beta)=(1,1)
.

3.2
Limit laws for the estimated clustering coefficient

In this section, we focus on the prediction of the clustering coefficient statistic (see Chapter 4 of
Kolaczyk (2009)
) of the population network
G_{N}
. The clustering coefficient is a widely used measure of cohesion within the network. Various researchers have focused on the estimation of the clustering coefficient through network sampling (cf.
Bliss et al. (2014)
,
Lee et al. (2006)
,
Illenberger and Flötteröd (2012)
,
Hardiman and Katzir (2013)
,
Iwasaki and Shudo (2018)
). To the best our knowledge, the asymptotic distribution of the sample based clustering coefficient estimate has not been studied in the existing literature, under any sort of framework or under any type of sampling scheme. According to
Bianconi (2018)
(cf. p. 17), the clustering coefficient of
G_{N}
can be written as

\displaystyle\Gamma_{N}\equiv\frac{3\times\text{(number of triangles in ${G}_{N}$)}}{\text{(number of distinct paths of length two in ${G}_{N}$)}}=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{S_{N}(\mathbb{K}_{3})}{S_{N}(\mathbb{K}_{1,2})}\displaystyle&\text{if $S_{N}(\mathbb{K}_{1,2})>0$,}\\
0&\text{otherwise,}.\end{cases}

(3.23)

where
S_{N}(\mathbb{K}_{1,2})
and
S_{N}(\mathbb{K}_{3})
denote the number of wedges and triangles in the population network
G_{N}
. The induced and ego-centric sample based estimates of the clustering coefficient are defined as

\displaystyle\widehat{\Gamma}_{N,l}=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{\widehat{S}_{N,l}(\mathbb{K}_{3})/{f_{l}(p_{N}:\mathbb{K}_{3})}}{\widehat{S}_{N,l}(\mathbb{K}_{1,2})/{f_{l}(p_{N}:\mathbb{K}_{1,2})}}\displaystyle&\quad\text{if $\widehat{S}_{N,l}(\mathbb{K}_{1,2})>0$,}\\
0&\quad\text{otherwise,}\end{cases},\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for $l=1,2$.}

(3.24)

where,
\widehat{S}_{N,l}(\mathbb{K}_{1,2})/f_{l}(p_{N}:\mathbb{K}_{1,2})
and
\widehat{S}_{N,l}(\mathbb{K}_{3})/f_{l}(p_{N}:\mathbb{K}_{3})
,
l=1,2
, (cf. (
3.5
)) are Horvitz-Thompson estimators of
S_{N}(\mathbb{K}_{1,2})
and
S_{N}(\mathbb{K}_{3})
,
f_{l}(p_{N}:\mathbb{K}_{1,2})
and
f_{l}(p_{N}:\mathbb{K}_{3})
have been defined in (
3.6
). Define the map
\psi(x,y)=x/y
, for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
and
y\neq 0
. The design unbiasedness of the Horvitz Thompson estimator implies
\mathbf{E}(\widehat{S}_{N,l}(H))=f_{l}(p_{N}:H)\cdot\mathbf{E}(S_{N}(H))
, for any choice of
H
. Thus, if
\widehat{S}_{N,l}(\mathbb{K}_{1,2})>0
, then we can use Taylor expansion, to write

\displaystyle N^{\beta}\cdot\left(\widehat{\Gamma}_{N,l}-{\Gamma}_{N}\right)=\frac{a_{N,l}\cdot\sigma_{N,l}(\mathbb{K}_{3})}{N^{(3-3\beta)}\cdot f_{l}(p_{N}:\mathbb{K}_{3})}\cdot T_{N,l}(\mathbb{K}_{3})+\frac{b_{N,l}\cdot\sigma_{N,l}(\mathbb{K}_{1,2})}{N^{(3-2\beta)}\cdot f_{l}(p_{N}:\mathbb{K}_{1,2})}\cdot T_{N,l}(\mathbb{K}_{1,2})+R_{N},

(3.25)

where,
a_{N,l}
and
b_{N,l}
depend on the model parameters, and involve the partial derivatives of
\psi(x,y)
,
R_{N}
is a remainder term,
T_{N,l}(H)
and
\sigma_{N,l}(H)
,
l=1,2
, are defined in (
1.3
) and (
3.12
). The limit law of
\left(\widehat{\Gamma}_{N,l}-\widehat{\Gamma}_{N}\right)
,
l=1,2
, is decided by the limiting distribution of the linear combination of
T_{N,1}(\mathbb{K}_{1,2})
and
T_{N,l}(\mathbb{K}_{3})
in (
3.25
). As it turns out, for certain values of
(\alpha,\beta)
, the triangle based term in this linear combination becomes the stochastically dominant term, and the wedge based term has negligible contribution. In the induced case, this happens if
(\alpha,\beta)\in R_{1,3}\cup R_{1,4}\cup R_{1,5}
and in the ego-centric case, the same situation arises if
(\alpha,\beta)\in R_{2,3}\cup R_{2,4}\cup R_{2,5}\cup R_{2,6}
(see blue shaded area in Figures
3
(a) and
3
(b)). For all other choices of
(\alpha,\beta)
, both the triangle and wedge based terms in (
3.25
) contribute equally to the limiting behavior of this linear combination. In the induced case, this happens if
(\alpha,\beta)\in R_{1,1}\cup R_{1,2}=C_{1,1}(\mathbb{K}_{3})
(see Figure
3
(a) and Figure
1
(a)). Similarly in the ego-centric case, the same situation is observed if
(\alpha,\beta)\in R_{2,1}\cup R_{2,2}=C_{2,1}(\mathbb{K}_{3})
(see Figure
3
(b) and Figure
2
(b)). If the
(\alpha,\beta)
pair increases further, beyond the regions shown in Figures
3
(a) and
3
(b) (in the induced and ego-centric cases, respectively), then the triangle based term in the linear combination (in (
3.25
)) continues to be the dominant term, but it does not lead to any non-degenerate limit law. Note that the wedge (
\mathbb{K}_{1,2}
) and triangle (
\mathbb{K}_{3}
) graphs, which are involved in the definition of the clustering coefficient, satisfy Assumption
(A.5)
. In order to state our main result, we define the following regions of sparsity levels,

(a) Sub-regions
R_{1,j}
,
j=1,\ldots,5
, in the induced case.

(b) Sub-regions
R_{2,j}
,
j=1,\ldots,6
, in the ego-centric case.

Figure 3
:
Figure showing various sub-regions arising during the estimation of the clustering coefficient (see (
3.26
) for details). The point
(\alpha,\beta)=(0,0)
and all yellow shaded areas give rise to Gaussian limit laws which are influenced by both wedge and triangle counts. Blue shaded areas give rise to Gaussian limit laws which are influenced only by the triangle counts. Poisson based limit laws arise on the red solid lines and on the black filled dot at
(\alpha,\beta)=(0,1)
. See Theorems
3.5
and
3.6
for details.

\left.\begin{aligned} &R_{1,1}=R_{2,1}=\left\{(\alpha,\beta):\alpha=\beta=0\right\},\\
&R_{1,2}=\left\{(\alpha,\beta):\alpha\in(0,1),~\alpha+\frac{\beta}{2/3}\leq 1\right\},~R_{2,2}=\left\{(\alpha,\beta):\alpha\in(0,1),\frac{\alpha}{2}+\frac{\beta}{2/3}\leq 1,~\alpha+\beta\leq 1\right\},\\
&R_{1,3}=\left\{(\alpha,\beta):\alpha\in(0,1),~\alpha+\frac{\beta}{2/3}>1,\alpha+\beta<1\right\},~R_{2,3}=\left\{(\alpha,\beta):\beta<\frac{1}{2},\alpha+\beta>1,\alpha+\frac{\beta}{2}<1\right\},\\
&R_{1,4}=\left\{(\alpha,\beta):\alpha\in(0,1),~\alpha+\beta=1\right\},~R_{2,4}=\left\{(\alpha,\beta):\beta\geq\frac{1}{2},\frac{\alpha}{2}+\frac{\beta}{2/3}>1,\frac{\alpha}{3/2}+\beta<1\right\},\\
&R_{1,5}=\left\{(\alpha,\beta):\alpha=0,\beta=1\right\},~~R_{2,5}=\left\{(\alpha,\beta):\alpha\in\left(0,3/4\right),\frac{\alpha}{3/2}+\beta=1\right\}\hskip 9.24994pt\text{and}\\
&R_{2,6}=\left\{(\alpha,\beta):\alpha=0,\beta=1\right\}.\end{aligned}\right\}

(3.26)

Recall the definition of
\mathbf{C}=((c_{u,v}:1\leq u,v\leq K))
and
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{K}
from (
2.5
) and Assumption
(A.2)
. Define the constants,

\displaystyle\theta_{1}=\sum_{u,v,w=1}^{K}c_{u,v}c_{u,w}\cdot\lambda_{u}\lambda_{v}\lambda_{w},\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\theta_{2}=\sum_{u,v,w=1}^{K}c_{u,v}c_{u,w}c_{v,w}\cdot\lambda_{u}\lambda_{v}\lambda_{w}.

(3.27)

Recall the definition of
p_{N}
from (
2.6
). Note, when
\alpha=0
,
p_{N}=c
, where
c\in(0,1)
. When
\alpha>0
, then
p_{N}=c/N^{\alpha}
, where
c>0
. We define the following asymptotic variance and asymptotic mean expressions, which are associated with limiting Gaussian and limiting Poisson based distributions, arising on the regions
R_{1,j}
,
j=1,\ldots,5
(cf. (
3.26
)), in the induced case:

\left.\begin{aligned} &\left[\rho_{1,1}(\mathbb{K}_{1,2},\mathbb{K}_{3})\right]^{2}=\sum_{u,v,w,x,y=1}^{K}\pi_{u,v}\pi_{u,w}\cdot\left\{\frac{\theta_{2}^{2}}{\theta_{1}^{4}}\cdot\frac{c^{5}(1-c)}{c^{6}}\cdot\left\{\pi_{u,x}\pi_{u,y}+4\pi_{u,x}\pi_{x,y}+4\pi_{v,x}\pi_{x,y}\right\}+\right.\\
&\quad\left.{}+\frac{1}{\theta_{1}^{2}}\cdot\frac{9c^{5}(1-c)}{c^{6}}\cdot\pi_{v,w}\pi_{u,x}\pi_{x,y}\pi_{y,u}-\frac{2\theta_{2}}{\theta_{1}^{3}}\cdot\frac{c^{5}(1-c)}{c^{6}}\cdot\left\{3\pi_{u,x}\pi_{x,y}\pi_{y,u}+6\pi_{v,x}\pi_{x,y}\pi_{y,v}\right\}\right\}\cdot\lambda_{u}\lambda_{v}\lambda_{w}\lambda_{x}\lambda_{y},\end{aligned}\right.

(3.28)

\displaystyle\left[\rho_{1,2}(\mathbb{K}_{1,2},\mathbb{K}_{3})\right]^{2}=\frac{1}{c\theta_{1}^{2}}\sum_{u,v,w,x,y=1}^{K}c_{u,v}c_{u,w}\left\{\frac{\theta_{2}^{2}}{\theta_{1}^{2}}\left\{c_{u,x}c_{u,y}+4c_{u,x}c_{x,y}+4c_{v,x}c_{x,y}\right\}+\right.

\displaystyle\left.{}+9\cdot c_{v,w}c_{u,x}c_{x,y}c_{y,u}-\frac{2\theta_{2}}{\theta_{1}}\cdot\left\{3c_{u,x}c_{x,y}c_{y,u}+6c_{v,x}c_{x,y}c_{y,v}\right\}\right\}\cdot\lambda_{u}\lambda_{v}\lambda_{w}\lambda_{x}\lambda_{y},

(3.29)

\left[\rho_{1,3}(\mathbb{K}_{3})\right]^{2}=\frac{\theta_{2}}{c^{3}\theta_{1}^{2}}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\kappa_{1,4}(\mathbb{K}_{3})=c^{3}\theta_{2}.

(3.30)

The subscript
j
(in
\rho_{1,j}
or
\kappa_{1,j}
) in the above expressions indicate that these asymptotic variance or mean expressions are associated with the limit law of the estimated clustering coefficient on the region
R_{1,j}
, for
j=1,\ldots,5
(cf. (
3.26
)). Similarly, the use of both the triangle (
\mathbb{K}_{3}
) and wedge (
\mathbb{K}_{1,2}
) symbols indicates that the Gaussian limit law is influenced by both the triangle and wedge based terms in (
3.25
), while if only the triangle symbol is used, it indicates that the limit law is influenced only by the triangle based term in (
3.25
). Similar notational convention will be used in the ego-centric case. Now we describe the main result about the limit law for the estimated clustering coefficient in the induced case.

Theorem 3.5

(Limit laws for the estimated clustering coefficient in the induced case)
.

Suppose that Assumptions
(A.1)
,
(A.2)
,
(A.3)
and
(A.4)
hold. Recall the definitions of
\theta_{1,N}
and
\theta_{2,N}
in (
3.27
) and the asymptotic variance expressions
[{\rho_{1,1}(\mathbb{K}_{1,2},\mathbb{K}_{3})}]^{2}
,
[{\rho_{1,2}(\mathbb{K}_{1,2},\mathbb{K}_{3})}]^{2}
,
[{\rho_{1,3}(\mathbb{K}_{3})}]^{2}
, from (
3.28
), (
3.29
) and (
3.30
). Define the independent Poisson random variables,
U_{1,4}
,
U_{1,5,1}
and
U_{1,5,2}
, with means
\kappa_{1,4}(\mathbb{K}_{3})
(cf. (
3.30
)),
c^{3}\cdot\kappa_{1,4}(\mathbb{K}_{3})
and
(1-c^{3})\cdot\kappa_{1,4}(\mathbb{K}_{3})
, respectively, where
c
is defined in (
2.6
). Recall the definitions of the regions
R_{1,j}
,
j=1,\ldots,5
, in (
3.26
). Then,

\displaystyle r_{N,1}(\alpha,\beta)\cdot\left(\widehat{\Gamma}_{N,1}-\Gamma_{N}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\rightarrow}}V_{1,\alpha,\beta},

(3.31)

where,

\left.\begin{aligned} r_{N,1}(\alpha,\beta)=\sqrt{N}\quad&\text{and}\quad V_{1,\alpha,\beta}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}N\left(0,{\left[\rho_{1,1}(\mathbb{K}_{1,2},\mathbb{K}_{3})\right]}^{2}\right),&\text{if $(\alpha,\beta)\in R_{1,1}$,}\\
r_{N,1}(\alpha,\beta)=N^{\displaystyle\beta+(1-\alpha)/2\displaystyle}\quad&\text{and}\quad V_{1,\alpha,\beta}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}N\left(0,\left[\rho_{1,2}(\mathbb{K}_{1,2},\mathbb{K}_{3})\right]^{2}\right),&\text{if $(\alpha,\beta)\in R_{1,2}$,}\\
r_{N,1}(\alpha,\beta)=N^{\beta+3(1-\alpha-\beta)/2}\quad&\text{and}\quad V_{1,\alpha,\beta}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}N\left(0,{\left[\rho_{1,3}(\mathbb{K}_{3})\right]}^{2}\right),&\text{if $(\alpha,\beta)\in R_{1,3}$,}\\
r_{N,1}(\alpha,\beta)=N^{\beta}\quad&\text{and}\quad V_{1,\alpha,\beta}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\frac{U_{1,4}-\kappa_{1,4}(\mathbb{K}_{3})}{\theta_{1}},&\text{if $(\alpha,\beta)\in R_{1,4}$,}\\
r_{N,1}(\alpha,\beta)=N\quad&\text{and}\quad V_{1,\alpha,\beta}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\frac{(1-c^{3})\cdot U_{1,5,1}-c^{3}\cdot U_{1,5,2}}{\theta_{1}},&\text{if $(\alpha,\beta)\in R_{1,5}$.}\end{aligned}\right\}

(3.32)

Similarly to the induced case, we define some asymptotic variance and mean expressions which will be used to define the limit law of the estimated clustering coefficient in the ego-centric case:

\displaystyle{\left[\rho_{2,1}(\mathbb{K}_{1,2},\mathbb{K}_{3})\right]}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u,v,\\
w,x,y=1\end{subarray}}^{K}\pi_{u,v}\pi_{u,w}\left\{\frac{\theta_{2}^{2}}{\theta_{1}^{4}}\cdot\frac{\left\{c(1-c)^{3}(1+c)^{2}\cdot\pi_{u,x}\pi_{u,y}+4c^{2}{(1-c)}^{3}(1+c)\cdot\pi_{u,x}\pi_{x,y}+4c^{3}{(1-c)}^{3}\cdot\pi_{v,x}\pi_{x,y}\right\}}{{\left(c+(1-c)\cdot c^{2}\right)}^{2}}\right.

\displaystyle\left.{}+\frac{1}{\theta_{1}^{2}}\cdot\frac{36c^{3}{(1-c)}^{3}\cdot\pi_{v,w}\pi_{u,x}\pi_{x,y}\pi_{y,u}}{{(3c^{2}-2c^{3})}^{2}}\right.

\displaystyle\left.{}-\frac{2\theta_{2}}{\theta_{1}^{3}}\cdot\frac{\left\{6c^{2}{(1-c)}^{3}(1+c)\cdot\pi_{u,x}\pi_{x,y}\pi_{y,u}+12c^{3}{(1-c)}^{3}\cdot\pi_{v,x}\pi_{x,y}\pi_{y,v}\right\}}{(c+c{(1-c)}^{2})\cdot(3c^{2}-2c^{3})}\right\}\cdot\lambda_{u}\lambda_{v}\lambda_{w}\lambda_{x}\lambda_{y},

(3.33)

\displaystyle{\left[\rho_{2,1}(\mathbb{K}_{1,2},\mathbb{K}_{3})\right]}^{2}=\frac{1}{c\theta_{1}^{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u,v,\\
w,x,y=1\end{subarray}}^{K}c_{u,v}c_{u,w}c_{u,x}c_{u,y}\left\{\frac{\theta_{2}^{2}}{\theta_{1}^{2}}+4\cdot c_{v,w}c_{x,y}-\frac{4\theta_{2}}{\theta_{1}}c_{x,y}\right\}\cdot\lambda_{u}\lambda_{v}\lambda_{w}\lambda_{x}\lambda_{y},

(3.34)

{\left[\rho_{2,3}(\mathbb{K}_{3})\right]}^{2}=\frac{1}{9c^{2}\theta_{1}^{2}}\cdot\sum_{u,v,w,x=1}^{K}c_{u,v}c_{v,w}c_{w,u}c_{u,x}c_{w,x}\cdot\lambda_{u}\lambda_{v}\lambda_{w}\lambda_{x},\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt{\left[\rho_{2,4}(\mathbb{K}_{3})\right]}^{2}=\frac{\theta_{2}}{3c^{2}\theta_{1}^{2}}.

(3.35)

Theorem 3.6

(Limit laws for the estimated clustering coefficient in the ego-centric case)
.

Suppose that Assumptions
(A.1)
,
(A.2)
,
(A.3)
and
(A.4)
hold. Recall the definitions of
\theta_{1,N}
and
\theta_{2,N}
in (
3.27
) and the asymptotic variance expressions
{[\rho_{2,1}(\mathbb{K}_{1,2},\mathbb{K}_{3})]}^{2}
,
{[\rho_{2,2}(\mathbb{K}_{1,2},\mathbb{K}_{3})]}^{2}
,
{[\rho_{2,3}(\mathbb{K}_{3})]}^{2}
,
{[\rho_{2,4}(\mathbb{K}_{3})]}^{2}
defined in (
3.33
), (
3.34
), (
3.35
). Define the independent Poisson random variables,
U_{2,5}
,
U_{2,6,1}
and
U_{2,6,2}
, with means
3c^{2}\cdot\theta_{2}
,
(3c^{2}-c^{3})\cdot\theta_{2}
and
\{1-(3c^{2}-c^{3})\}\cdot\theta_{2}
, respectively, where
c
is defined in (
2.6
). Define the constant
\delta_{c}=(3c^{2}-c^{3})
. Recall the definitions of the regions
R_{2,j}
,
j=1,\ldots,6
, in (
3.26
). Then

\displaystyle r_{N,2}(\alpha,\beta)\cdot\left(\widehat{\Gamma}_{N,2}-\Gamma_{N}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\rightarrow}}V_{2,\alpha,\beta},

(3.36)

where,

\left.\begin{aligned} r_{N,2}(\alpha,\beta)=\sqrt{N}\quad&\text{and}\quad V_{2,\alpha,\beta}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}N\left(0,{\left[\rho_{2,1}(\mathbb{K}_{1,2},\mathbb{K}_{3})\right]}^{2}\right),&\text{if $(\alpha,\beta)\in R_{2,1}$,}\\
r_{N,2}(\alpha,\beta)=N^{\displaystyle\beta+(1-\alpha)/2\displaystyle}\quad&\text{and}\quad V_{2,\alpha,\beta}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}N\left(0,\left[\rho_{2,2}(\mathbb{K}_{1,2},\mathbb{K}_{3})\right]^{2}\right),&\text{if $(\alpha,\beta)\in R_{2,2}$,}\\
r_{N,2}(\alpha,\beta)=N^{\displaystyle\beta+(2-2\alpha-\beta)/2\displaystyle}\quad&\text{and}\quad V_{2,\alpha,\beta}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}N\left(0,{\left[\rho_{2,3}(\mathbb{K}_{3})\right]}^{2}\right),&\text{if $(\alpha,\beta)\in R_{2,3}$,}\\
r_{N,2}(\alpha,\beta)=N^{\displaystyle\beta+(3-2\alpha-3\beta)/3\displaystyle}\quad&\text{and}\quad V_{2,\alpha,\beta}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}N\left(0,{\left[\rho_{2,4}(\mathbb{K}_{3})\right]}^{2}\right),&\text{if $(\alpha,\beta)\in R_{2,4}$,}\\
r_{N,2}(\alpha,\beta)=N^{\beta}\quad&\text{and}\quad V_{2,\alpha,\beta}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\frac{U_{2,5}-3c^{2}\cdot\theta_{2}}{\theta_{1}},&\text{if $(\alpha,\beta)\in R_{2,5}$,}\\
r_{N,2}(\alpha,\beta)=N\quad&\text{and}\quad V_{2,\alpha,\beta}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\frac{\{1-\delta_{c}\}\cdot U_{2,6,1}-\delta_{c}\cdot U_{2,6,2}}{\theta_{1}},&\text{if $(\alpha,\beta)\in R_{2,6}$.}\end{aligned}\right\}

(3.37)

In the ego-centric case, the limiting distribution of the estimated triangle count remains unknown on the boundary of the region
R_{2,3}
in Figure
3
(b) (also see the region
U
in Figure
1
(b)). For this reason, the limit law of the estimated clustering coefficient also remains unknown on this region. The variance expressions obtained in Theorem
3.5
and Theorem
3.6
are complicated, especially when
\alpha
or
\beta
is zero. This happens due to the various complicated covariance terms, which arise while finding the limiting distribution of the linear combination of the wedge and triangle based terms in (
3.25
).

4
Simulation results and real data analysis

If
T_{N,l}(H)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\rightarrow}}F
, where
F
is a c.d.f., then we can construct the following asymptotic two-sided level
(1-\eta)

prediction interval
(PI) for the unknown population subgraph density
N^{-R}\cdot S_{N}(H)
(where
R=|V(H)|
),

\displaystyle J_{\eta,l,F}(H)\equiv\left[\frac{\left(\widehat{S}_{N,l}(H)-\xi_{1-\eta/2}\cdot\sigma_{N,l}(H)\right)}{N^{R}\cdot f_{l}(p_{N}:H)},\frac{\left(\widehat{S}_{N,l}(H)-\xi_{\eta/2}\cdot\sigma_{N,l}(H)\right)}{N^{R}\cdot f_{l}(p_{N}:H)}\right],

(4.1)

for
l=1,2
, where
\xi_{\eta}
denotes the
\eta
-quantile of the distribution
F
,
f_{l}(p_{N}:H)
are defined in (
3.6
) and
\sigma_{N,l}(H)
are defined in (
3.12
). A similar approach can be used to construct an asymptotic two-sided PI for the unknown clustering coefficient
\Gamma_{N}
.

In our simulation study, we will study the empirical coverage of
J_{\eta,l,F}(H)
for various choices of
H
(edge, wedge and triangle) and do the same with the PI for the clustering coefficient, in both induced and ego-centric cases. We will focus on various model and sampling sparsity levels. The expressions of
\sigma^{2}_{N,l}(H)
for the above choices of
H
are provided in Appendix
Appendix E: Variance expressions for some common target subgraphs
, and the expressions of asymptotic variance for the estimated clustering coefficient is provided in Section
3.2
. Appendix
Appendix E: Variance expressions for some common target subgraphs
also contains the variance expressions for
\sigma^{2}_{N,l}(\mathbb{K}_{1,R-1})
and
\sigma^{2}_{N,l}(\mathbb{K}_{R})
, for
l=1,2
.

The main challenge is to estimate the unknown variance term
\sigma^{2}_{N,l}(H)
, since it depends on unknown model parameters like the limiting matrix
\mathbf{C}
(cf. (
2.5
)), the limiting class size proportions
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{K}
(cf. Assumption
(A.2)
), the unknown edge probabilities
\pi_{N,u,v}
(cf. (
2.2
)) and also on the model sparsity level
\beta
. If
F
is a Poisson based limit law, then
\xi_{\eta}
will also depend on these model parameters. Throughout the simulation study, we will assume that the sparsity level of the model (
\beta
) is
known
. Similarly, the sampling sparsity level
\alpha
will be
known
. Since our results show sharp transitions from Gaussian to Poisson-based limit laws as the levels of
(\alpha,\beta)
change, even an accurate estimate of
\beta
(if available), can lead us to the use of an incorrect limit law for obtaining the PI’s. In fact,
\beta
is an extraneous quantity that is used to quantify the sparsity in
G_{N}
, and it is not a model (SBM) parameter. We construct the proposed PI’s (cf. (
4.1
)) in two different situations: when all above described model parameters are known, and when all of them are unknown and estimated from the sampled data.

We use the
Bethe-hessian clustering
algorithm for community detection proposed by
Saade et al. (2014)
, which uses spectral clustering to detect communities within a network.. We obtain an estimate
\widehat{K}
of
K
by using the largest estimated class label. Using the estimated class label information
\{\widehat{\alpha}_{i}:1\leq i\leq N,W_{N,i}=1\}
from sampled nodes, we construct a naive estimate of the class-wise edge probabilities,

\displaystyle\widehat{\pi}_{k,m}(l)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{(i,j)\in I_{l,N}}Y_{i,j}\cdot\mathbf{1}\left(\widehat{\alpha}_{i}=k,\widehat{\alpha}_{j}=m\right)}{\sum_{(i,j)\in I_{l,N}}\mathbf{1}\left(\widehat{\alpha}_{i}=k,\widehat{\alpha}_{j}=m\right)},\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for $l=1,2$, for all $1\leq k,m\leq\widehat{K}$,}

where
I_{l,N}
is defined in (
2.9
). The above mentioned community detection algorithm can not be used on the data set obtained from the ego-centric network formation approach. Thus, in case of
l=2
(ego-centric), we make use of
\widehat{K}
and class label estimates obtained from
\mathcal{Y}_{1,N}
(cf. (
2.9
)), but
\widehat{\pi}_{k,m}(2)
are obtained by using the ego-centric sample based dataset
\mathcal{Y}_{2,N}
.

When model parameters are estimated using sampled network data
\mathcal{Y}_{l,N}
,
l=1,2
, this dataset consists of
dependent
entries, as the sampling design effect is included. Due to this dependence, the usual theoretical properties (consistency, etc.) of the estimates of
K
or class labels (obtained through community detection algorithms) or estimates of edge probabilities may not hold. As a result, the ‘estimated’ version
\widehat{J}_{\eta,l,F}(H)
of the PI in (
4.1
), obtained by plugging in these estimated parameters,
does not
provide any theoretical guarantee about its nominal coverage level, even asymptotically.

Five different simulation settings are explored, each focusing on a specific choice of model and sampling sparsity level. In all cases, empirical coverages of the proposed PI’s were obtained by using 2000 Monte-Carlo replications. Each Monte-Carlo replication consists of initially generating
\mathbf{Y}_{N}
from the true model (SBM), independently selecting a sample of nodes from
[N]
by Bernoulli sampling, and then observing the sampled data (through the induced/ego-centric approach). This process is repeated during every Monte-Carlo replication. This allows us to replicate the joint model and design based inferential framework. Throughout the simulation study we used a two class SBM (
K=2
), with equal class size proportions
\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}=1/2
, while the edge probabilities, population size
N
and the node sampling probability are changed.

(S.1)

Dense network and dense sampling:
In this case, we set
\alpha=\beta=0
. We use
\pi_{N,1,1}=0.20
,
\pi_{N,1,2}=0.05
,
\pi_{N,2,2}=0.10
and
N=20000
. We use
p_{N}\in\{0.01,0.05\}
(cf. (
2.6
)), which implies that the expected number of sampled nodes will be 200 and 1000, respectively. The empirical coverages are provided in Table
.

(S.2)

Sparse network and dense sampling:
In this case we set
\beta=1/2
,
\alpha=0
,
\pi_{N,1,1}=0.00506
,
\pi_{N,1,2}=0.00206
,
\pi_{N,2,2}=0.00901
and
N=100000
. We use
p_{N}\in\{0.01,0.05\}
, which implies that the expected number of sampled nodes will be 1000 and 5000, respectively. The results are provided in Table
.

(S.3)

Sparse network and sparse sampling:
We use
\beta=1/2
and
\alpha=1/4
,
\pi_{N,1,1}=0.00112
,
\pi_{N,1,2}=0.000112
,
\pi_{N,2,2}=0.000447
and
N=200000
. We use
p_{N}=0.0002=(0.00423)\cdot N^{-1/4}
. The expected number of sampled nodes is approximately 845. Table
displays the results and only focuses on the case where the parameters are known.

(S.4)

Extreme model sparsity and dense sampling:
We focus on
H=\mathbb{K}_{2}
(edge, with
R=2
,
T=1
) and consider the highest possible model sparsity level
\beta=2~(=R/T)
. We use
\alpha=0
. Edge probabilities are
\pi_{N,1,1}=3\times 10^{-7}
,
\pi_{N,1,2}=10^{-7}
and
\pi_{N,2,2}=2\times 10^{-7}
. We use
N=10^{6}
(1 million) and
p_{N}\in\{0.01,0.05\}
. The expected number of sampled nodes will be 10000 and 50000, respectively. Table
displays the results and only focuses on the case where the true parameters are known. The quantiles of the limit law (cf. Theorem
3.4
(a)) are approximated by Monte-Carlo simulation.

(S.5)

Dense model and extremely sparse sampling:
We focus on the edge graph
H=\mathbb{K}_{2}
. The model is dense and the sampling is extremely sparse (
\beta=0
and
\alpha=1
). The edge probabilities are
\pi_{N,1,1}=0.2
,
\pi_{N,1,2}=0.05
and
\pi_{N,2,2}=0.10
. We use
N=2000000
(2 million) and
p_{N}=0.000025=50/N
. The expected number of sampled nodes is 50. We focus on ego-centric network formation and the Poisson-based limit law of
T_{N,2}(\mathbb{K}_{2})
is provided in Theorem
3.4
(c). We found that the nominal 95% two-sided asymptotic PI in (
4.1
) (with true model parameters) provided an empirical coverage of
0.935
, with average width
0.057
.

The results in Tables
,
,
and
and the results for simulation setup (S.5) show excellent empirical coverages for the proposed PI’s in all the above five simulation scenarios, even when the model parameters are estimated on the basis of the sampled data. This strongly supports our theoretical results and also illustrates their applicability. The computations were carried out on a 64 core server with 128 GB RAM. The code was written in Rcpp and it was further optimized for increased speed through the use of generative AI. All code, data and outputs are available on the github page
https://github.com/AnirbanM13/subgraph-count-estimate
.

Remark
9

(Evaluation of the variance expression)
.

For arbitrary choices of
H
, the expression for
\sigma^{2}_{N,l}(H)
can involve a sum over numerous terms, each requiring the construction of an union graph formed by two overlapping isomorphic copies of
H
. It is nearly impossible to do this manually for even for relatively simple choices of
H
. But this can be achieved through the use of a computer program by using the following expressions. Fix any
t\in[R]
and consider the set of vectors
{[R]}_{<,t}
(cf. (
3.1
)), and the collection
\mathcal{S}_{t}
of all permutations of
\{1,\ldots,t\}
. For any
\mathbf{k}\in{[R]}_{<,t}
, let
B(\mathbf{k},t)
denote the
t
-vertex subgraph of
H
, induced by the vertices in
A(\mathbf{k})
. Recall the definition of
f_{2}(p:H)
(cf. (
3.7
)), the node sampling probability
p_{N}
(cf. (
2.6
)), the class size proportions
\{\lambda_{N,k}:k\in[K]\}
described in (
2.4
) and the edge probability matrix
\bm{\Pi}_{N}=((\pi_{N,u,v}:1\leq u,v\leq K))
defined in (
2.2
). For any
t\in[R]
, any
\mathbf{j},\mathbf{k}\in{[R]}_{<,t}
and any permutation
\xi\in\mathcal{S}_{t}
, define the quantity,

\displaystyle\Theta_{N,t}(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{k},\xi:\bm{\Pi}_{N},\lambda_{N,1},\ldots,\lambda_{N,K},H)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\in{[K]}^{R}}\prod_{\{r,s\}\in E(H)}\pi_{N,u_{r},u_{s}}\prod_{\{r,s\}\in E(H)\setminus E(B(\mathbf{k},t))}\pi_{N,v_{r},v_{s}}\prod_{r=1}^{R}\lambda_{N,u_{r}}\prod_{s\in[R]\setminus A(\mathbf{k})}\lambda_{N,v_{s}}\prod_{r=1}^{t}\mathbf{1}\left(u_{j_{r}}=v_{k_{\xi(r)}}\right).

(4.2)

Then, for any target subgraph
H
, we can write (as
N\rightarrow\infty
),

\displaystyle\sigma^{2}_{N,1}(H)

\displaystyle\asymp\sum_{t=1}^{R}\left(p^{2R-t}_{N}-p^{2R}_{N}\right)N^{2R-t}\sum_{\mathbf{j},\mathbf{k}\in{[R]}_{<,t}}\sum_{\xi\in\mathcal{S}_{t}}\Theta_{N,t}(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{k},\xi:\bm{\Pi}_{N},\lambda_{N,1},\ldots,\lambda_{N,K},H).

(4.3)

Now, recall the definition of
\mathcal{M}_{t}(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{k},\xi)
from (
3.13
). Let
(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{1},\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{2})\in\mathcal{M}_{t}(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{k},\xi)
, with
A(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{1})=[R]
and
A(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{1})\cup A(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{2})=\{1,\ldots,2R-t\}
. Consider the union graph
H(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{1})\cup H(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{2})
and denote it by
H(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{k},\xi)
, where these graphs are created according to the method described above in (
3.12
). Let
\mathcal{D}\left(H(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{k},\xi)\right)
denote the collection of all vertex covers of
H(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{k},\xi)
. Then, for any target subgraph
H
satisfying Assumption
(A.5)
we can write (as
N\rightarrow\infty
),

\displaystyle\sigma^{2}_{N,2}(H)\asymp\sum_{t=1}^{R}N^{2R-t}\sum_{\mathbf{j},\mathbf{k}\in{[R]}_{<,t}}\sum_{\xi\in\mathcal{S}_{t}}

\displaystyle\left[\sum_{D\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{k},\xi)}p^{|D|}_{N}{(1-p_{N})}^{2R-t-|D|}-f^{2}_{2}(p_{N}:H)\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\times\Theta_{N,t}(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{k},\xi:\bm{\Pi}_{N},\lambda_{N,1},\ldots,\lambda_{N,K},H).

(4.4)

For the benefit of practitioners, the relevant code for computing these variance expressions for any choice of
H
is provided on the github page
https://github.com/AnirbanM13/subgraph-count-estimate
. They require inputs of (either true or estimated) model parameters,
K
,
\bm{\Pi}_{N}
,
\{\lambda_{N,k}\}
, the adjacency matrix of
H
the value of
p_{N}
and the choice of
\beta
. The code can be used for target graphs
H
with
R=7
vertices. The computational burden increases rapidly as
R
increases. The github page also includes a code for verifying Assumption
(A.5)
for a given choice of
H
.

4.1
Analysis of a real data set

In this section, we apply our proposed methodology on a real data set. We focus on the
2004 US Political blogs networks data
1
1

1

https://public.websites.umich.edu/~mejn/netdata/
. The data set is compiled by
Adamic and Glance (2005)
and has been used earlier in the context of community detection (cf.
Le and Levina (2022)
,
Amini et al. (2013)
). Each node in the data set represents blogs focused on US politics, and the edges are the hyperlinks between these blogs. After removing isolated nodes from the original dataset, the population network consists of
N=1224
nodes. The original data had directed edges. We transform the data to create an undirected population network in the following manner. If
Z_{i,j}
denotes the
(i,j)
-th entry of the adjacency matrix of the original (directed) network, we create a new symmetric adjacency matrix with the
(i,j)
-th entry
Y_{i,j}=Y_{j,i}=\max\{Z_{i,j},Z_{j,i}\}
. Our data analysis will be based on this newly created adjacency matrix. Each blog is manually labeled
liberal
(denoted as class 1) or
conservative
(denoted as class 2). This manual labeling is available from this
source
. Labeling
liberal
or
conservative
automatically defines two classes in the population, with
N_{1}=588
and
N_{2}=636
. The average degree and edge density of the population network are
27.312
and
0.02233
respectively. Using the class label information for all
N
nodes in the population, we can obtain the
true
edge connection probabilities,
\pi_{1,1}=0.0423
,
\pi_{1,2}=0.0042
and
\pi_{2,2}=0.0388
.

\LL

A
single
Bernoulli sample of nodes was drawn from the population network. We used
p_{N}=0.1
, and the expected number of sampled nodes is
\approx 122
. We use
\alpha=0
and
\beta=0
. The following estimates of the model parameters were obtained from the induced sample,
\widehat{K}=2
,
\widehat{\lambda}_{N,1}=0.2061
,
\widehat{\lambda}_{N,2}=0.7938
,
\widehat{\pi}_{1,1}=0.1908
,
\widehat{\pi}_{1,2}=0.0064
and
\widehat{\pi}_{2,2}=0.0211
. Table
shows the true edge, wedge and triangle counts, and the true clustering coefficient. It also shows the corresponding sampling based estimates in the induced and ego-centric cases and the resulting 95% PI’s. The above found estimates of the model parameters were used to construct these PI’s. The real data analysis shows that the proposed 95% PI’s contain the values of the true population summary statistics.

Conclusion

This article provides a rigorous study of the effects of network sampling, especially when sampling is carried out under resource constraints, and the population network can be
dense or sparse. The effects of both induced and ego-centric network formation are explored. A model based setup is used and the asymptotic theory for estimated subgraph counts is developed a joint model and design based framework. Our results provide deep insights into the effects of network sampling under resource constraints and its complex relation with the sparsity level in the population network and the structure of the target subgraph. We have also developed Poisson based limit laws for estimated subgraph counts at more extreme sparsity levels and showed that various types of Poisson based limit laws can arise. Most importantly, the criteria for obtaining a Gaussian limit law also turn out to be necessary. In addition, we have developed limit laws for the estimated clustering coefficient statistic under similar settings. This is a completely new and important finding in the context of the clustering coefficient statistic. Our results are potentially applicable in any situation where the population network exhibits a block structure and theory can be possibly extended for more complicated network models. We believe that this article makes an important and meaningful contribution in the area of network sampling based inference.

This appendix contains the following materials. Appendix

contains some notation and definitions that have been used in our proofs. Appendix

contains proofs of our main results. Appendix
Appendix C: Results from other sources
contains results from other sources that have been used in our proofs. Appendix
Appendix D: Supplementary lemmas
contains some supplementary results that are required to prove the main results. Appendix
Appendix E: Variance expressions for some common target subgraphs
contains the variance expressions
\sigma^{2}_{N,l}(H)
for specific choices of
H
, like the edge, wedge and triangle graphs and also the expressions for any complete and star graph.

Appendix A: Notation and definitions

Firstly, we introduce some definitions and notation. Some of these definitions are repeated for the sake of completeness. For any positive integer
m
, we write
[m]=\{1,\ldots,m\}
to denote the set of the first
m
positive integers. For any two sequences of positive real numbers
\{a_{N}:N\geq 1\}
and
\{b_{N}:N\geq 1\}
, we write
a_{N}\asymp b_{N}
, if
a_{N}/b_{N}\rightarrow 1
, as
N\rightarrow\infty
. Similarly, we write
a_{N}\sim b_{N}
, if there exist constants
0<m_{1}<m_{2}<\infty
, and an integer
N_{0}\geq 1
, such that
m_{1}<a_{N}/b_{N}<m_{2}
, for all
N\geq N_{0}
. Define the following collections of
R
dimensional vectors,

\displaystyle{\displaystyle[N]}^{R}=\left\{\mathbf{s}={\left(s_{1},\ldots,s_{R}\right)}^{T}:s_{i}\in[N],\ \text{for all $i\in[R]$}\right\},\hskip 8.50012pt{[N]}_{R}=\left\{\mathbf{s}\in{[N]}^{R}:\text{$s_{1},\ldots,s_{R}$ are distinct}\right\},\ \text{and}

(E.1)

\displaystyle{\displaystyle[N]}_{<,R}=\left\{\mathbf{s}\in{[N]}_{R}:s_{1}<s_{2}<\cdots<s_{R}\right\}.

For any vector
\mathbf{u}={(u_{1},\ldots,u_{t})}^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{t}
, we write

\displaystyle A(\mathbf{u})

\displaystyle=\{a\in\mathbb{R}:\exists\ \text{some $i\in[t]$, such that, $u_{i}=a$}\}=\text{the set of unique components of $\mathbf{u}$.}

(E.2)

For example, consider any
\mathbf{s}={(s_{1},\ldots,s_{R})}^{T}\in{[N]}_{R}
, where
{[N]}_{R}
is defined in (
3.1
). Then
A(\mathbf{s})=\{s_{1},\ldots,s_{R}\}
. If
\mathbf{s}_{1}={(1,2,3)}^{T}
and
\mathbf{s}_{2}={(2,1,3)}^{T}
, then
A(\mathbf{s}_{1})=A(\mathbf{s}_{2})=\{1,2,3\}
.
Let
t
be a positive integer and consider another integer
s\in\{1,\ldots,t\}
. Consider a
s
dimensional vector
\mathbf{i}={(i_{1},\ldots,i_{s})}^{T}\in{[t]}_{<,s}
. For any
\mathbf{u}={(u_{1},\ldots,u_{t})}^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{t}
, we write the
s
-dimensional sub-vector of
\mathbf{u}
,
{(\mathbf{u})}_{\mathbf{i}}={\left(u_{i_{1}},\ldots,u_{i_{s}}\right)}^{T}
, containing components of
\mathbf{u}
indexed by
\mathbf{i}
. For example, if
\mathbf{u}={(1,3,2,6)}^{T}
and
\mathbf{i}={(1,4,3)}^{T}
, then
\mathbf{u}_{\mathbf{i}}={(1,6,2)}^{T}
. Following Assumption
(A.2)
, define the
K
dimensional vector of class proportions and their limiting values,

\displaystyle\bm{\lambda}_{N}={(\lambda_{N,1},\ldots,\lambda_{N,K})}^{T}\hskip 8.50012pt\text{and}\hskip 8.50012pt\bm{\lambda}={(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{K})}^{T}.,

(E.3)

Recall that
\lambda_{N,i}
converges to
\lambda_{i}
for each
i\in[K]
(see Section
2
), for all
i\in[K]
. In addition, we write
\mathbb{N}_{0}=\{0,1,2,\ldots\}
as the set of all non-negative integers.

Now, we define some distance metrics on the space of all probability measures. Following
Dudley (2002)
(see p. 420), the Wasserstein distance between two
d
dimensional random vectors
\mathbf{X}
and
\mathbf{Y}
is defined as

\displaystyle d_{{W}}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y})=\sup\bigg\{\left|\mathbf{E}(h(\mathbf{X}))-\mathbf{E}(h(\mathbf{Y}))\right|:h\in\mathcal{A}_{W}\bigg\},

(E.4)

where,
\mathcal{A}_{W}=\{h\mid h:\mathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto\mathbb{R},~{\|{h}\|}_{L}\leq 1\}
,
{\|{h}\|}_{L}=\sup\{|h(\mathbf{x})-h(\mathbf{y})|/{\|{\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}}\|}_{1}:\mathbf{x}\neq\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\}
is the Lipschitz norm of the map
h
, and
{\|\mathbf{x}\|}_{1}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}|x_{j}|
, is the
\ell_{1}
norm for any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. The bounded Wasserstein distance (also known as the bounded Lipschitz distance) between two
d
dimensional random vectors
\mathbf{X}
and
\mathbf{Y}
is defined as (see p. 394
Dudley (2002)
),

\displaystyle d_{{BL}}\left(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y}\right)=\bigg\{\left|\mathbf{E}(h(\mathbf{X}))-\mathbf{E}(h(\mathbf{Y}))\right|:h\in\mathcal{A}_{BL}\bigg\},

(E.5)

where,
\mathcal{A}_{BL}=\{h\mid h:\mathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto\mathbb{R},~{\|{h}\|}_{L}+{\|{h}\|}_{\infty}\leq 1\}
,
{\|{h}\|}_{\infty}=\sup\{|h(\mathbf{x})|:\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\}
and
{\|{h}\|}_{L}
has been defined earlier. Obviously,
\mathcal{A}_{BL}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{W}
and
d_{BL}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y})\leq d_{W}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y})
. Convergence in
d_{W}
implies convergence in
d_{BL}
. The total variation distance between two real valued random variables
X
and
Y
is defined as

\displaystyle d_{{TV}}\left(X,Y\right)=\sup\bigg\{\left|\mathbf{E}(h(X))-\mathbf{E}(h(Y))\right|:h\in\mathcal{A}_{TV}\bigg\},

(E.6)

where,
\mathcal{A}_{TV}=\{h_{B}\mid B\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R})\}
, and
h_{B}(x)=\mathbf{1}(x\in B)
, for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
, and
\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R})
is the Borel sigma-field in
\mathbb{R}
.

Various constant terms will arise within the proofs of our main and supplementary results. Using appropriate notation, we will keep track of how these constant terms depend on all or some of the following quantities: the underlying sparsity levels
\alpha
and
\beta
, the underlying (limiting) model parameters
\mathbf{C},\bm{\lambda}
(see (
2.5
), (
E.3
)), the constant term
c
within
p_{N}
(see (
2.6
)), the target subgraph
H
and also on the network formation mechanism
l
(with
l=1
, for the induced case and
l=2
for the ego-centric case). If a constant
a_{1}
depends on the parameters
\alpha
,
\beta
,
H
,
\bm{\lambda}
,
\mathbf{C}
, and
l
, we write,
a_{1}=a_{1}(l,\alpha,\beta,H,\bm{\lambda},\mathbf{C})
. If
a_{1}
depends only on
\alpha,\beta
and
\bm{\lambda}
, we write
a_{1}=a_{1}(\alpha,\beta,\bm{\lambda})
. We will keep track of how the various constants arising through our proofs depend on all or some of the underlying parameters. But, for notational simplicity we will suppress this dependency. Similarly, certain statements will hold if the population network size
N
becomes large enough and exceeds a threshold
M_{1}
(or
M_{2},\ldots
). We will keep track of how each
M_{i}
depends on
(l,\alpha,\beta,H,\bm{\lambda},\mathbf{C})
(or a subset of them), but we will suppress the dependency for notational convenience. Sometimes, the same symbol or variable names may appear in the proof of different results, but they may denote or define different quantities within the different proofs. The meaning of this sort of symbol or variable will only be confined in that proof.

Appendix B: Proofs of main results

Proof of Theorem
3.1
.

For any
\mathbf{s}\in{[N]}_{R}
, define
Y_{N}(\mathbf{s}:H)=\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E(H)}Y_{\mathbf{s}_{i},\mathbf{s}_{j}}
and
\widetilde{W}_{N,l}(\mathbf{s}:H)=\left(W_{N,l}(\mathbf{s}:H)-f_{l}(p_{N}:H)\right)
, where
W_{N,l}(\mathbf{s}:H)=\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E(H)}h_{l}\left(W_{N,s_{i}},W_{N,s_{j}}\right)
, and
f_{l}(p:H)
is defined in (
3.6
) for
l=1,2
. We can write

\displaystyle T_{N,l}(H)=\frac{1}{\sigma_{N,l}(H)}\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in[N]_{R}}\widetilde{W}_{N,l}(\mathbf{s}:H)\cdot Y_{N}(\mathbf{s}:H),

(E.7)

where,
\sigma^{2}_{N,l}(H)=\mathbf{Var}\left(\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in[N]_{R}}\widetilde{W}_{N,l}(\mathbf{s}:H)\cdot Y_{N}(\mathbf{s}:H)\right)
,
l=1,2
. This is a sum of dependent random variables. For any
\mathbf{s}\in{[N]}_{R}
, the corresponding component
\widetilde{W}_{N,l}(\mathbf{s}:H)\cdot Y_{N}(\mathbf{s}:H)
in the above sum has its own dependency neighborhood
N(\mathbf{s})=\{\mathbf{s}_{1}\in{[N]}_{R}:|A(\mathbf{s})\cap A(\mathbf{s}_{1})|\geq 1\}
. Let
Z\sim N(0,1)
. Following the arguments presented in
Barbour et al. (1989)
(cf. Section 3), the existence of the dependency neighborhood structure for each
\mathbf{s}\in{[N]}_{R}
in (
E.7
) allows us to apply Theorem 6.32 of
Janson et al. (2000)
(see Theorem
C.1
) on the summands involved in (
E.7
), and obtain the following upper bound,

\displaystyle d_{1}\left(T_{N,l}(H),Z\right)\leq\frac{1}{\sigma^{3}_{N,l}(H)}\sum_{\mathbf{s}_{1}\in{[N]}_{R}}\sum_{\mathbf{s}_{2}\in N(\mathbf{s}_{1})}\sum_{\mathbf{s}_{3}\in N(\mathbf{s}_{1})}\left[\mathbf{E}\left|\prod_{i=1}^{3}\widetilde{W}_{N,l}(\mathbf{s}_{i}:H)\right|\cdot\mathbf{E}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{3}Y_{N}(\mathbf{s}_{i}:H)\right)\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 8.50012pt\left.{}+\mathbf{E}\left|\widetilde{W}_{N,l}(\mathbf{s}_{1}:H)\cdot\widetilde{W}_{N,l}(\mathbf{s}_{2}:H)\right|\cdot\mathbf{E}|\widetilde{W}_{N,l}(\mathbf{s}_{3}:H)|\cdot\mathbf{E}\left(Y_{N}(\mathbf{s}_{1}:H)\cdot Y_{N}(\mathbf{s}_{2}:H)\right)\cdot\mathbf{E}(Y_{N}(\mathbf{s}_{3}:H))\right]

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{1}{\sigma^{3}_{N,l}(H)}\left(A_{N,l,1}+A_{N,l,2}\right),\hskip 8.50012pt\text{for all $N\geq 1$ and for both $l=1,2$.}

(E.8)

For any
k_{1},k_{2}\in[R]
, we define the following collection of vectors,

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{k_{1},k_{2}}

\displaystyle=\Bigl\{(\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2},\mathbf{s}_{3}):\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2},\mathbf{s}_{3}\in[N]_{R},\,\lvert A(\mathbf{s}_{2})\cap A(\mathbf{s}_{1})\rvert=k_{1},\,\lvert A(\mathbf{s}_{3})\cap(A(\mathbf{s}_{1})\cup A(\mathbf{s}_{2}))\rvert=k_{2}\Bigr\}.

(E.9)

From the definitions of the neighborhood
N(\mathbf{s})
and the set
\mathcal{M}_{k_{1},k_{2}}
, it follows that

\Bigl\{(\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2},\mathbf{s}_{3}):\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2},\mathbf{s}_{3}\in[N]_{R},\,\mathbf{s}_{2}\in N(\mathbf{s}_{1}),\,\mathbf{s}_{3}\in N(\mathbf{s}_{1})\Bigr\}\subseteq\bigcup_{k_{1},k_{2}=1}^{R}\mathcal{M}_{k_{1},k_{2}}.

As a result, to obtain an upper bound on
A_{N,l,1}
,
l=1,2
(cf. (
E.8
)), which involves non-negative expected values, we can enlarge the sum using the above set inclusion, and write

\displaystyle A_{N,l,1}\leq\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{R}\sum_{k_{2}=1}^{R}\sum_{(\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2},\mathbf{s}_{3})\in\mathcal{M}_{k_{1},k_{2}}}\mathbf{E}\left|\prod_{i=1}^{3}\widetilde{W}_{N,l}(\mathbf{s}_{i}:H)\right|\cdot\mathbf{E}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{3}Y_{N}(\mathbf{s}_{i}:H)\right).

(E.10)

Similarly, we can obtain an upper bound for
A_{N,l,2}
by extending the summation over the larger set
\cup_{k_{1},k_{2}}\mathcal{M}_{k_{1},k_{2}}
. Consider the expected value of the design based quantity in the r.h.s. of (
E.10
). Note that
\mathbf{E}(W_{N,l}(\mathbf{s}:H))=f_{l}(p_{N}:H)
, for any
\mathbf{s}\in{[N]}_{R}
, for both
l=1,2
. Thus, for any
\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2},\mathbf{s}_{3}\in{[N]}_{R}
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left|\prod_{i=1}^{3}\widetilde{W}_{N,l}(\mathbf{s}_{i}:H)\right|=\mathbf{E}\left|\prod_{i=1}^{3}\left(W_{N,l}(\mathbf{s}_{i}:H)-f_{l}(p_{N}:H)\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{E}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{3}W_{N,l}(\mathbf{s}_{i}:H)\right)+3f_{l}(p_{N}:H)\cdot\mathbf{E}\left(W_{N,l}(\mathbf{s}_{1}:H)\cdot W_{N,l}(\mathbf{s}_{2}:H)\right)+4{(f_{l}(p_{N}:H))}^{3},\hskip 8.50012pt\text{for $l=1,2$.}

(E.11)

Also note, if
(\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2},\mathbf{s}_{3})\in\mathcal{M}_{k_{1},k_{2}}
(cf. (
E.9
)), then

|A(\mathbf{s}_{1})\cup A(\mathbf{s}_{2})\cup A(\mathbf{s}_{3})|=|A(\mathbf{s}_{1})|+|A(\mathbf{s}_{2})\cap A(\mathbf{s}_{1})^{c}|+\left|A(\mathbf{s}_{3})\cap\left(A(\mathbf{s}_{1})\cup A(\mathbf{s}_{2})\right)^{c}\right|=(3R-k_{1}-k_{2}).

Now consider the induced case (
l=1
) and note that
f_{1}(p_{N}:H)=p_{N}^{R}
. Then, for any
(\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2},\mathbf{s}_{3})\in\mathcal{M}_{k_{1},k_{2}}
, the expected values in the r.h.s. of (
E.11
) can be written as,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{3}W_{N,1}(\mathbf{s}_{i}:H)\right)=p_{N}^{|A(\mathbf{s}_{1})\cup A(\mathbf{s}_{2})\cup A(\mathbf{s}_{3})|}=p_{N}^{3R-k_{1}-k_{2}},\hskip 8.50012pt{(f_{1}(p_{N}:H))}^{3}=p_{N}^{3R},

(E.12)

\displaystyle\text{and}\hskip 8.50012ptf_{1}(p_{N}:H)\cdot\mathbf{E}\left(W_{N,1}(\mathbf{s}_{1}:H)W_{N,l}(\mathbf{s}_{2}:H)\right)=p_{N}^{3R-k_{1}}.

Adding up the quantities in the r.h.s. of (
E.11
) (for
l=1
) and using the expressions obtained above in (
E.12
), it now follows that for any
k_{1},k_{2}\in[R]
and
(\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2},\mathbf{s}_{3})\in\mathcal{M}_{k_{1},k_{2}}
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left|\prod_{i=1}^{3}\widetilde{W}_{N,1}(\mathbf{s}_{i}:H)\right|\leq 8\mathbf{E}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{3}W_{N,1}(\mathbf{s}_{i}:H)\right)=8\cdot p_{N}^{3R-k_{1}-k_{2}}.

(E.13)

A similar argument can be carried out for the components within
A_{N,1,2}
(cf. (
E.8
)) and using (
E.12
), we can claim,

\mathbf{E}\left|\widetilde{W}_{N,1}(\mathbf{s}_{1}:H)\cdot\widetilde{W}_{N,1}(\mathbf{s}_{2}:H)\right|\cdot\mathbf{E}|\widetilde{W}_{N,1}(\mathbf{s}_{3}:H)|\leq 8\cdot p_{N}^{3R-k_{1}-k_{2}},\hskip 8.50012pt\text{if $(\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2},\mathbf{s}_{3})\in\mathcal{M}_{k_{1},k_{2}}$.}

Next, we consider the ego-centric case (
l=2
). Consider any
(\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2},\mathbf{s}_{3})\in\mathcal{M}_{k_{1},k_{2}}
(cf. (
E.9
)) and let
H(\mathbf{s}_{i})
,
i=1,2,3
, denote an isomorphic copy of
H
with vertex set
V(H(\mathbf{s}_{i}))=A(\mathbf{s}_{i})
and edge set
E(H(\mathbf{s}_{i}))=\{\{s_{i,r},s_{i,s}\}:\{r,s\}\in E(H)\}
,
i=1,2,3
. Let
\mathcal{D}(\cup_{i=1}^{3}H(\mathbf{s}_{i}))
denote the collection of all vertex covers of
\cup_{i=1}^{3}H(\mathbf{s}_{i})
, and let
\mathcal{D}_{min}(\cup_{i=1}^{3}H(\mathbf{s}_{i}))
denote the collection of all minimum vertex covers within
\mathcal{D}(\cup_{i=1}^{3}H(\mathbf{s}_{i}))
. Let
|\mathcal{D}(\cup_{i=1}^{3}H(\mathbf{s}_{i}))|
denote the total number of vertex covers of
\cup_{i=1}^{3}H(\mathbf{s}_{i})
. Note that,
|\mathcal{D}(\cup_{i=1}^{3}H(\mathbf{s}_{i}))|\leq{|\mathcal{D}(H)|}^{3}
, where
|\mathcal{D}(H)|
denotes the total number of vertex covers of
H
. Due to Lemma
D.8
part (b), there exists a minimum vertex cover
D^{\star}(\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2},\mathbf{s}_{3})\in\mathcal{D}_{min}(\cup_{i=1}^{3}H(\mathbf{s}_{i}))
, such that

\displaystyle|D^{\star}(\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2},\mathbf{s}_{3})|

\displaystyle\geq 3\tau(H)-\min\{\tau(H),k_{1}\}-\min\{\tau(H),k_{2}\}.

(E.14)

Now, using (
3.7
), the inequalities in (
E.14
) and the minimum vertex cover
D^{\star}(\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2},\mathbf{s}_{3})
defined above, for any
(\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2},\mathbf{s}_{3})\in\mathcal{M}_{k_{1},k_{2}}
we can write the first term on the r.h.s. of (
E.11
) as

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left(\prod_{r=1}^{3}W_{N,2}(\mathbf{s}_{r}:H)\right)=\mathbf{E}\left(\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E(H(\mathbf{s}_{1})\cup H(\mathbf{s}_{2})\cup H(\mathbf{s}_{3}))}\max\{W_{N,i},W_{N,j}\}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{D\in\mathcal{D}({\cup_{i=1}^{3}H(\mathbf{s}_{i})})}p_{N}^{|D|}\left(1-p_{N}\right)^{3R-|D|}

\displaystyle=\sum_{D\in\mathcal{D}_{\min}({\cup_{i=1}^{3}H(\mathbf{s}_{i})})}p_{N}^{|D|}\cdot(1-p_{N})^{3R-|D|}+\sum_{D\in\mathcal{D}(\cup_{i=1}^{3}H(\mathbf{s}_{i}))\setminus\mathcal{D}_{\min}(\cup_{i=1}^{3}H(\mathbf{s}_{i}))}p_{N}^{|D|}\cdot(1-p_{N})^{3R-|D|}

\displaystyle\leq p_{N}^{|D^{\star}(\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2},\mathbf{s}_{3})|}\left\{(1-p_{N})^{3R-|D^{\star}(\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2},\mathbf{s}_{3})|}\cdot\left|\mathcal{D}_{\min}(\cup_{i=1}^{3}H(\mathbf{s}_{i}))\right|\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 17.00024pt\left.{}+\sum_{D\in\mathcal{D}(\cup_{i=1}^{3}H(\mathbf{s}_{i}))\setminus\mathcal{D}_{\min}(\cup_{i=1}^{3}H(\mathbf{s}_{i}))}p_{N}^{|D|-|D^{\star}(\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2},\mathbf{s}_{3})|}\cdot(1-p_{N})^{3R-|D|}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq p_{N}^{|D^{\star}(\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2},\mathbf{s}_{3})|}\cdot\left|\mathcal{D}(\cup_{i=1}^{3}H(\mathbf{s}_{i}))\right|

\displaystyle\leq p_{N}^{{}3\tau(H)-\min\{\tau(H,k_{1})-\min\{\tau(H),k_{2}\}\}}{\left|\mathcal{D}(H)\right|}^{3}.

(E.15)

Using Lemma
D.8
part (a),
for any
(\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2},\mathbf{s}_{3})\in\mathcal{M}_{k_{1},k_{2}}
, we can put the following upper bounds on the different expected values in r.h.s. of (
E.11
),

\displaystyle{\displaystyle(f_{2}(p_{N}:H))}^{3}\leq p^{3\tau(H)}_{N}{\left|\mathcal{D}(H)\right|}^{3},\hskip 8.50012pt\text{and}

(E.16)

\displaystyle f_{2}(p_{N}:H)\cdot\mathbf{E}(W_{N,2}(\mathbf{s}_{1}:H)\cdot W_{N,2}(\mathbf{s}_{2}:H))\leq p_{N}^{\{3\tau(H)-\min\{\tau(H),k_{1}\}\}}{\left|\mathcal{D}(H)\right|}^{3}.

We can now combine the bounds in (
E.15
), (
E.16
) and use them in (
E.11
) to claim that

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left|\prod_{r=1}^{3}\widetilde{W}_{N,2}(\mathbf{s}_{r}:H)\right|

\displaystyle\leq 8\cdot\mathbf{E}\left(\prod_{r=1}^{3}W_{N,2}(\mathbf{s}_{r}:H)\right)\leq 8\cdot{\left|\mathcal{D}(H)\right|}^{3}\cdot p^{3\tau(H)-\min\{\tau(H),k_{1}\}-\min\{\tau(H),k_{2}\}}_{N},

(E.17)

for any
(\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2},\mathbf{s}_{3})\in\mathcal{M}_{k_{1},k_{2}}
. To obtain the above described exponent of
p_{N}
(in the r.h.s. of (
E.17
)), we have used the fact that
p_{N}<1
, and
k_{1}
,
k_{2}
, and
\tau(H)
are strictly positive. Similarly to the induced case, we can get the same upper bound for the components within
A_{N,2,2}
(cf. (
E.8
)),

\mathbf{E}\left|\widetilde{W}_{N,2}(\mathbf{s}_{1}:H)\cdot\widetilde{W}_{N,2}(\mathbf{s}_{2}:H)\right|\cdot\mathbf{E}|\widetilde{W}_{N,2}(\mathbf{s}_{3}:H)|\leq 8\cdot{\left|\mathcal{D}(H)\right|}^{3}\cdot p^{3\tau(H)-\min\{\tau(H),k_{1}\}-\min\{\tau(H),k_{2}\}}_{N},

Combining (
E.13
) and (
E.17
), and defining
a_{1}=a_{1}(H)=\max\left\{1,\left|\mathcal{D}(H)\right|^{3}\right\}
, we have (from (
E.8
)), the following bound for both
l=1
and
l=2
,

\displaystyle A_{N,l,1}\leq 8\cdot a_{1}\cdot\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{R}\sum_{k_{2}=1}^{R}p_{N}^{\displaystyle e_{l}(k_{1},k_{2}:H)\displaystyle}\sum_{(\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2},\mathbf{s}_{3})\in\mathcal{M}_{k_{1},k_{2}}}\mathbf{E}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{3}Y_{N}(\mathbf{s}_{i}:H)\right),

(E.18)

where,

\displaystyle e_{l}(k_{1},k_{2}:H)=\begin{cases}3R-k_{1}-k_{2}&\text{if $l=1$,}\\
3\tau(H)-\min(\tau(H),k_{1})-\min(\tau(H),k_{2})&\text{if $l=2$.}\end{cases}

(E.19)

In order to control the expected values for the model based terms in (
E.10
), we define the following sub-collection of vectors within the collection
\mathcal{M}_{k_{1},k_{2}}
(cf. (
E.9
)),

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{k_{1},k_{2}}=\Bigl\{(\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2},\mathbf{s}_{3})\in\mathcal{M}_{k_{1},k_{2}}:

\displaystyle|E(H(\mathbf{s}_{1})\cap H(\mathbf{s}_{2}))|=m(k_{1}:H),

\displaystyle|E(H(\mathbf{s}_{3}))\cap(E(H(\mathbf{s}_{1})\cup H(\mathbf{s}_{2})))|=m(k_{2}:H)\Bigr\}.

Note that, the collection
\mathcal{O}_{k_{1},k_{2}}
is non-empty. Since there is a sub-collection

\mathcal{O}_{0,k_{1},k_{2}}=\{(\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2},\mathbf{s}_{3})\in\mathcal{M}_{k_{1},k_{2}}:|E(H(\mathbf{s}_{1})\cap H(\mathbf{s}_{2}))|=m(k_{1}:H),|E(H(\mathbf{s}_{1})\cap H(\mathbf{s}_{3}))|=m(k_{2}:H)\}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{k_{1},k_{2}},

and
\mathcal{O}_{0,k_{1},k_{2}}
is non-empty.
Thus, if
(\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2},\mathbf{s}_{3})\in\mathcal{O}_{k_{1},k_{2}}
, then for these choices of
(\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2},\mathbf{s}_{3})
, the graphs
H(\mathbf{s}_{1})\cap H(\mathbf{s}_{2})
and
H(\mathbf{s}_{3})\cap\left(H(\mathbf{s}_{1})\cup H(\mathbf{s}_{2})\right)
achieve their highest possible edge count sizes (see Lemma
D.5
). As a result, if
(\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2},\mathbf{s}_{3})\in\mathcal{O}_{k_{1},k_{2}}
, then splitting the edges within
\cup_{i=1}^{3}H(\mathbf{s}_{i})
into disjoint collections, we can write

\displaystyle|E(H(\mathbf{s}_{1})\cup H(\mathbf{s}_{2})\cup H(\mathbf{s}_{3}))|

\displaystyle=|E(H(\mathbf{s}_{1}))|+|E(H(\mathbf{s}_{2}))|-|E(H(\mathbf{s}_{1})\cap E(H(\mathbf{s}_{2}))|+|E(H(\mathbf{s}_{3}))|-|E(H(\mathbf{s}_{3})\cap\left(H(\mathbf{s}_{1})\cup H(\mathbf{s}_{2})\right)|

\displaystyle=T+\left(T-m(k_{1}:H)\right)+\left(T-m(k_{2}:H)\right)=3T-m(k_{1}:H)-m(k_{2}:H).

(E.20)

And if
(\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2},\mathbf{s}_{3})\in\mathcal{M}_{k_{1},k_{2}}\setminus\mathcal{O}_{k_{1},k_{2}}
, then the number of edges within these graph intersections must be smaller than their maximum possible values,
i.e.
,

\displaystyle|E(H(\mathbf{s}_{1})\cap E(H(\mathbf{s}_{2}))|

\displaystyle=m(k_{1}:H)-\delta_{1}(\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2},\mathbf{s}_{3})\hskip 8.50012pt\text{and}

\displaystyle|E(H(\mathbf{s}_{3})\cap\left(H(\mathbf{s}_{1})\cup H(\mathbf{s}_{2})\right)|

\displaystyle=m(k_{2}:H)-\delta_{2}(\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2},\mathbf{s}_{3}),

where,
\delta_{1}(\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2},\mathbf{s}_{3})
and
\delta_{2}(\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2},\mathbf{s}_{3})
are some non-negative integers, and at least one of them is strictly positive. This implies, if
(\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2},\mathbf{s}_{3})\in\mathcal{M}_{k_{1},k_{2}}\setminus\mathcal{O}_{k_{1},k_{2}}
, then

\displaystyle|E(H(\mathbf{s}_{1})\cup H(\mathbf{s}_{2})\cup H(\mathbf{s}_{3}))|=T+\left(T-\{m(k_{1}:H)-\delta_{1}(\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2},\mathbf{s}_{3})\}\right)+\left(T-\{m(k_{2}:H)-\delta_{2}(\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2},\mathbf{s}_{3})\}\right)

\displaystyle=3T-m(k_{1}:H)-m(k_{2}:H)+\delta_{1}(\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2},\mathbf{s}_{3})+\delta_{2}(\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2},\mathbf{s}_{3})

\displaystyle\leq 3T-m(k_{1}:H)-m(k_{2}:H)+\delta_{k_{1},k_{2}},

(E.21)

where,
\delta_{k_{1},k_{2}}=2\cdot\max_{(\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2},\mathbf{s}_{3})\in\mathcal{M}_{k_{1},k_{2}}}\left\{\delta_{1}(\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2},\mathbf{s}_{3}),\delta_{2}(\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2},\mathbf{s}_{3})\right\}
, is a non-negative integer that depends on
k_{1}
and
k_{2}
, and it is bounded above by
\max_{1\leq t\leq R}{m(t:H)}=T
, which is fixed and finite. Also note that from Assumption
(A.3)
in (
2.5
), we can claim that there exists a positive integer
M_{1}
(possibly depending on
\beta
), such that for all
N\geq M_{1}
,

N^{\beta}\max_{1\leq i,j\leq K}\pi_{N,i,j}\leq 2\cdot\max_{1\leq i,j\leq K}c_{i,j}=2\cdot c_{\max},\hskip 8.50012pt\text{(say).}

For
N\geq M_{1}
, using (
E.20
) and (
E.21
) the sum over
\mathcal{M}_{k_{1},k_{2}}
in the r.h.s. of (
E.18
) can be written as

\displaystyle\sum_{(\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2},\mathbf{s}_{3})\in\mathcal{M}_{k_{1},k_{2}}}\mathbf{E}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{3}Y_{N}(\mathbf{s}_{i}:H)\right)=\sum_{(\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2},\mathbf{s}_{3})\in\mathcal{O}_{k_{2},k_{2}}}\mathbf{E}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{3}Y_{N}(\mathbf{s}_{i}:H)\right)+\sum_{(\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2},\mathbf{s}_{3})\in\mathcal{O}^{c}_{k_{2},k_{2}}}\mathbf{E}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{3}Y_{N}(\mathbf{s}_{i}:H)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2},\mathbf{s}_{3})\in\mathcal{O}_{k_{2},k_{2}}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E\left(\cup_{i=1}^{3}H(\mathbf{s}_{i})\right)}\pi_{N,\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j}}+\sum_{(\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2},\mathbf{s}_{3})\in\mathcal{O}^{c}_{k_{2},k_{2}}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E\left(\cup_{i=1}^{3}H(\mathbf{s}_{i})\right)}\pi_{N,\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{(\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2},\mathbf{s}_{3})\in\mathcal{O}_{k_{2},k_{2}}}N^{-\beta(3T-m(k_{1}:H)-m(k_{2}:H))}{\left(N^{\beta}\max_{1\leq i,j\leq K}\pi_{N,i,j}\right)}^{3T-m(k_{1}:H)-m(k_{2}:H)}

\displaystyle\hskip 17.00024pt{}+\sum_{(\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2},\mathbf{s}_{3})\in\mathcal{O}^{c}_{k_{2},k_{2}}}N^{-\beta(3T-m(k_{1}:H)-m(k_{2}:H))}N^{-\beta\cdot\delta_{k_{1},k_{2}}}\left(N^{\beta}\max_{1\leq i,j\leq K}\pi_{N,i,j}\right)^{{}3T-m(k_{1}:H)-m(k_{2}:H)+\delta_{k_{1},k_{2}}}

\displaystyle\leq{\left(2c_{\max}\right)}^{3T-m(k_{1}:H)-m(k_{1}:H)+\delta_{k_{1},k_{2}}}\sum_{(\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2},\mathbf{s}_{3})\in\mathcal{M}_{k_{1},k_{2}}}N^{-\beta(3T-m(k_{1}:H)-m(k_{2}:H))}

\displaystyle=a_{2}\cdot N^{3R-k_{1}-k_{2}-\beta(3T-m(k_{1}:H)-m(k_{2}:H))},

(E.22)

where
a_{2}=a_{2}(\beta,H,\mathbf{C})=\max_{k_{1},k_{2}\in[R]}\left\{{\left(2c_{\max}\right)}^{3T-m(k_{1}:H)-m(k_{1}:H)+\delta_{k_{1},k_{2}}}\right\}
, is a positive constant depending on
\beta
, the graph
H
, and
\mathbf{C}
. Using similar arguments as used earlier, the same upper bound can be used for the term
\mathbf{E}(Y_{N}(\mathbf{s}_{1}:H)\cdot Y_{N}(\mathbf{s}_{2}:H))\cdot\mathbf{E}(Y_{N}(\mathbf{s}_{3}:H))
, within the term
A_{N,l,2}
in (
E.8
). Then, combining (
E.18
), and a similar upper bound for
A_{N,l,2}
and using the definition of
g_{l,\alpha,\beta,H}(t)
,
t_{l}(\alpha,\beta;H)
and
e_{l}(k_{1},k_{2}:H)
,
l=1,2
, defined in (
1.5
) and (
E.19
), we can claim that there exists a positive integer
M_{2}
, depending on
l,\alpha,\beta
and
H
, such that for all
N\geq M_{2}
,

\displaystyle A_{N,l,1}\leq a_{1}\cdot a_{2}\cdot\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}=1}^{R}N^{3R-k_{1}-k_{2}-\alpha\cdot e_{l}(k_{1},k_{2})-\beta(2T-m(k_{1}:H)-m(k_{2}:H))}

\displaystyle=a_{1}\cdot a_{2}\cdot N^{-3\cdot g_{l,\alpha,\beta,H}(R)}\sum_{k=1}^{R}N^{2\cdot g_{l,\alpha,\beta,H}(k)}\leq a_{1}\cdot a_{2}\cdot N^{{}-3g_{l,\alpha,\beta,H}\left(R\right)}N^{2g_{l,\alpha,\beta,H}\left(t_{l}(\alpha,\beta;H)\right)}\{1+o(1)\}.

(E.23)

From Lemma
D.12
, there exists a positive integer
M_{3}
, depending on
l,\alpha,\beta
and the graph
H
, such that for all
N\geq M_{3}

\displaystyle a_{3}\leq\frac{\sigma^{3}_{N,l}(H)}{\left[N^{-2g_{l,\alpha,\beta,H}(R)}\cdot N^{g_{l,\alpha,\beta,H}(t_{l}(\alpha,\beta;H))}\right]^{3/2}}\leq a_{4},

(E.24)

where
a_{3}=\left[L(l,\alpha,\beta,H,\bm{\lambda},\mathbf{C})\right]^{3/2}
and
a_{4}=\left[U(l,\alpha,\beta,H,\bm{\lambda},\mathbf{C})\right]^{3/2}
, are finite positive constants defined in Lemma
D.12
. Recall that Assumption
(A.5)
is used obtained (
E.24
) in case of
l=2
or egocentric network formation. Then, combining (
E.23
) and the lower bound of (
E.24
), for
N\geq\max\{M_{1},M_{2},M_{3}\}
, we get the following bound

\displaystyle A_{N,l,1}\leq\frac{a_{1}\cdot a_{2}}{a_{3}}\cdot\displaystyle N^{~\displaystyle\left\{g_{l,\alpha,\beta,H}\left(t_{l}(\alpha,\beta;H)\right)/2\right\}\displaystyle}.

A similar upper bound is also available for the term
A_{N,l,2}
using the previously stated arguments. As a result,

\displaystyle d_{1}\!\left(T_{N,l}(H),~Z\right)\leq b_{l}(H)\,N^{~\displaystyle\left\{g_{l,\alpha,\beta,H}\left(t_{l}(\alpha,\beta;H)\right)/2\right\}\displaystyle},\hskip 8.50012pt\text{for $l=1,2$ and $N\geq\max\{M_{1},M_{2},M_{3}\}$,}

where,
b_{l}(H)=\frac{a_{1}\cdot a_{2}}{a_{3}}
,
l=1,2
, depend on
H
, the model parameters, and the sparsity levels
(\alpha,\beta)
. This completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Corollary
3.2
.

For part (a), we have already established this statement in the ego-centric case (for
l=2
) in (
3.11
). The induced case (for
l=1
) follows similarly. To prove part (b), note that from Theorem (
3.1
), we know

\displaystyle d_{1}\left(T_{N,l}(H),Z\right)\leq b_{l}(H)\cdot\displaystyle N^{~\displaystyle\left\{g_{l,\alpha,\beta,H}\left(t_{l}(\alpha,\beta;H)\right)/2\right\}\displaystyle},

for large enough
N
. When
(\alpha,\beta)\in C_{l}(H)
(cf. (
1.6
), (
1.7
)), the exponent of
N
in the above bound is negative, which implies that stated order relation holds for
l=1,2
. Part (c) is trivial consequence of the fact that convergence in
d_{1}
distance implies convergence in distribution.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.4
(a): Dense sampling.

In this case,
(\alpha,\beta)=(0,R/T)
. As a result
p_{N}=c/N^{\alpha}=c\in(0,1)
, for all
N\geq 1
. For
l=1,2
, define the random vectors

\displaystyle\mathbf{U}_{N,l}

\displaystyle=(U_{N,l,1},U_{N,l,2})^{T}=\bigl(\widehat{S}_{N,l}(H),\,S_{N}(H)-\widehat{S}_{N,l}(H)\bigr)^{T}=\left(\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in{[N]}_{R}}X_{l}^{1}(\mathbf{s}),\,\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in{[N]}_{R}}X_{l}^{2}(\mathbf{s})\right)^{T},~\text{where,}

(E.25)

\displaystyle X_{l}^{1}(\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle=\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E(H)}h_{l}(W_{N,s_{i}},W_{N,s_{j}})\,Y_{s_{i},s_{j}},\hskip 17.00024ptX_{l}^{2}(\mathbf{s})=\left(1-\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E(H)}h_{l}(W_{N,s_{i}},W_{N,s_{j}})\right)\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E(H)}Y_{s_{i},s_{j}}.

We develop a multivariate Poisson approximation for the vector
\mathbf{U}_{N,l}
using Theorem 2.6 of
Nualart and Zhang (2025)

(see Theorem
C.4
for details). The first step is to construct a size-biased coupling for
\mathbf{U}_{N,l}
, for
l=1,2
. For each
\mathbf{r}\in{[N]}_{R}
, we define the following random vectors,
